#7201. Senior Product Marketing Manager [IC4] at Sourcegraph

Our mission is to bring clarity and control to the world's most complex codebases. AI is accelerating code creation, but the infrastructure to understand, oversee, and evolve that code hasn't kept pace. Sourcegraph gives engineering organizations full visibility across their systems, precise context for their agents, and the ability to execute coordinated code changes at scale. As agentic development becomes the dominant engineering paradigm, we provide the context layer teams need to take control of their codebase.With Code Search, Deep Search, MCP, and Agentic Batch Changes, we deliver on that mission today - giving engineering teams and their AI tools the cross-repo context to navigate massive codebases with confidence, and the ability to make changes across hundreds of repositories at once.Companies like Stripe, Reddit, and Leidos rely on Sourcegraph to ship faster and with higher quality. We're backed by a16z, Sequoia, and Redpoint, and proud to operate as a globally distributed team that values high agency, direct communication, and customer love.If you want to build the infrastructure that lets every engineering team - and every agent they deploy - operate on their codebase with confidence, join us.

🌎 While we hire almost anywhere in the world, we have a preference for someone to reside in the following locations for this role. However, if you feel qualified, we welcome you to apply regardless of location. No matter what, working hours must overlap with Pacific Time for at least 25 hours/week.

We are hiring a Sr. Product Marketing Manager to help us tell the Sourcegraph story and scale the go-to-market for one of the most critical pieces of engineering infrastructure for the largest enterprises building the future.In this role, you will report to our Head of Marketing, and sit at the intersection of our go-to-market and technical teams to drive thought leadership, product leadership, and market leadership. Our customers are developers and engineering leaders at the largest and most successful companies in the world who rely on us to help them write better code, understand their codebases, secure their products, and adopt the newest and most impactful technology. You will translate deep technical capability into clear market narratives, turn product direction into launches that land, and ensure go-to-market is aligned, credible, and effective.Sourcegraph is a $50m ARR business and growing fast. We are doubling down on our core Search technology, including Deep Search, Code Search, Sourcegraph MCP and APIs for agents, and critical code migration and workflow engines, like Agentic Batch Changes for large-scale changes at scale. You will be responsible for the technical product marketing that will bring that story to life in the market, as well as ensuring alignment with and results from our Demand Gen and DevRel teams.We move fast and hold ourselves to an extremely high bar for product quality and Developer authenticity. If you’ve made a mark in product marketing for developers, talk to us.

You have a track record of success in driving and owning product marketing initiatives end-to-end. You’re equally comfortable shaping narratives that connect with our technical enterprise audience, enabling sales, and shipping launches that move the business forward. You understand the software development lifecycle and are excited about how AI is reshaping developer tools and workflows.Must-haves:

📊 This job is an IC4. You can read more about our job leveling philosophy in our Handbook.

💸 We pay you an above-average salary because we want to hire the best people who are fully focused on helping Sourcegraph succeed, not worried about paying bills. As an open and transparent company that values competitive compensation, our compensation ranges are visible to every single Sourcegraph teammate.

Your salary is determined by your pay band for the IC4 job level. For determining pay bands, we use a number of market and data-driven salary sources, along with your location zone, and target the high-end of the range to ensure we’re always paying above market regardless of where you live in the world. Both U.S. and international locations are divided into one of four zones, determined by the cost of labor index for each area. The salary for a successful candidate will be based on level, job-related skills, experience, qualifications, and location zone. Please note that the salaries below may be adjusted in the future.

#7198. Employers want AI skills. Here’s how colleges are adding them without new hires.

Ask a college president what students need to graduate ready for work, and AI is now near the top of the list. More than a third of entry-level jobs now require AI skills, nearly triple the share six months earlier. Yet 63% of faculty say recent graduates aren't prepared to use AI in the workplace.

Most academic leaders agree AI belongs in the curriculum. The harder question is who will teach it, especially at small, tuition-dependent institutions.

AI adds hurdles most new programs don't face:

So many institutions stall, and students graduate without the skills employers are hiring for.

Rize Education helps colleges lower costs and grow enrollment through a shared program model, letting them launch high-demand, career-focused programs faster and for far less than building them alone. Its two AI programs have been adopted 94 times across the network of institutions.

With more than 1,800 students enrolled through these courses, Rize Education can finally answer the question provosts ask most: do students actually value an online AI course?

Rize surveys students at the middle and end of every course. Here’s what they said:

Students rate these courses as highly as others. 89% of Applied AI responses and 83% of AI Literacy responses rate the course as good as or better than other online courses students have taken. Against on-campus courses, the figures are 76% and 73%.

Limited AI experience didn’t hold students back. AI Literacy students rated their prior familiarity with AI just 3.9 out of 7, yet rated their confidence in finishing with a good grade 6.2 out of 7.

A well-run course shapes the experience. Students gave their highest marks to clear assignment instructions, reasonable grading and helpful instructors. As one AI Literacy student put it: "This course is challenging but all of the assignments work together to give a clear, functional foundational knowledge of AI that can immediately be put to use in professional and private settings."

For more than 1,300 students, an AI course was their first Rize-powered course, a sign these programs draw students in rather than just checking a box.

Employers are asking for AI skills, and students are rating these courses as highly as anything else they take. The full results break down what 1,800 students said about both programs.

Read the full results from Rize's AI programs.

#7194. Researchers and faculty sue NSF over axing of its social science unit

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The federal agency said it would close the unit in fiscal 2027, in what the lawsuit described as a unilateral decision that violated Administrative Procedure Act.

Since April, NSF has “stopped funding opportunities for basic SBE research,” reassigned almost all of SBE's staff and cut ties with scientists who were working with the directorate, according to Monday’s lawsuit.

President Donald Trump also fired every member of SBE’s governing board, who plaintiffs said had been kept in the dark about the agency’s future since he retook office almost two years ago.

Their lawsuit argued that NSF breached the APA by failing to consider the outcome of doing away with SBE — namely, that the agency would be unable to fulfill its mandate to “initiate and support basic scientific research and programs” in the social sciences.

Each year, SBE distributes some $247 million in grants for research into the social, behavioral and economic sciences — nearly two-thirds of all federal grants in these fields, according to NSF.

“Without that funding, hundreds of promising research projects directed to the pressing problems of our time — from the use and effects of artificial intelligence to child development in the age of social media — will never proceed,” the lawsuit said. Thousands of people also will lose their jobs, it said.

The plaintiffs also said NSF has given no official rationale for why it decided to extinguish the directorate.

In response to questions from The Atlantic, a White House spokesperson said in a May statement that the Trump administration “is committed to cementing America’s dominance in cutting-edge technologies of the future — innovation that is being driven by advancements in hard sciences, not in ideologically-driven ‘social sciences.’”

But the administration never said why it is labeling entire fields such as psychology and economics as “ideologically driven,” the plaintiffs said in their lawsuit Monday. And even if it were to explain, they argued, the rationale would directly conflict with the statutes that created NSF, “which repeatedly direct the Foundation to support the social sciences.”

All of this has occurred with a significant power vacuum at the top levels of NSF. The agency has been without a permanent leader for more than a year and a half, after its last director, Sethuraman Panchanathan, resigned in April 2025. It is also without a deputy director.

Both positions require a presidential appointment and Senate confirmation. Trump’s pick for NSF director, former U.S. Department of Health and Human Services Deputy Secretary Jim O'Neill, has not been given a confirmation hearing.

NSF Chief of Staff Brian Stone, a defendant in Monday’s lawsuit, is currently performing the duties of the agency’s director. The vast majority of NSF's significant cuts and the corresponding tumult occurred during Stone’s tenure. He is set to retire at the end of the year.

“NSF cannot shut down the government’s largest source of funding for an entire scientific sector without a Director and without consulting the body Congress created to make such Decisions,” Monday’s lawsuit said. “And it certainly cannot do so with no explanation.”

Alongside two named college instructors, the organizations suing NSF are the Federation of Associations in Behavioral and Brain Sciences, the Society of Experimental Social Psychology, and the Social & Affective Neuroscience Society. The plaintiffs are represented by the advocacy nonprofit Democracy Forward.

#7191. Growth Copywriter at Winona

Winona is one of the leading telemedicine companies providing HRT for women in menopause. We’ve built all of our technology in house including an EHR, patient care admin system, patient portal, website, marketing technology etc. Winona has two compounding pharmacies and a team of in house physicians providing world-class care via a fully vertically integrated business model.

We are looking for a data-backed, empathetic Growth Copywriter to own the performance and optimization of our end-to-end messaging ecosystem. At Winona, copy isn’t just about sounding nice. It’s a measurable variable in our growth funnel. You will design and execute rapid copy experiments across our paid acquisition, intake flows, and lifecycle retention loops to help women confidently access bioidentical HRT and menopause care.

Full-Funnel Experimentation. Write copy hypotheses, design rapid A/B tests alongside growth and analytics teams, analyze performance data, and turn winning variations into a scalable messaging playbook.

Lifecycle Growth & Retention. Write, test, and optimize automated onboarding, activation, adherence, churn-save, and win-back sequences across email, SMS, and push notifications to drive LTV.

High-Intent Conversion Surfaces. Own the persuasive strategy and microcopy for landing pages, interactive quiz/intake flows, checkouts, and dynamic pricing/offer messaging.

Paid Acquisition at Scale. Generate volume-driven concepts, hooks, and static/video copy variants for Meta, Google, and YouTube, systematically iterating based on creative testing data.

Voice of Customer (VoC) Intelligence. Continuously mine product reviews, customer support tickets, intake data, and exit surveys to isolate deep qualitative insights and high-converting customer language.

AI-Augmented Workflows. Leverage and train Winona’s internal AI copywriting tools to scale production velocity, maintain brand consistency, and build automated variant pipelines without sacrificing quality.

5+ years of dedicated copywriting experience specializing in direct-response, conversion copywriting, or growth marketing.

DTC Telehealth, Wellness, or HealthTech experience is highly preferred. You understand how to write about sensitive health topics with clinical authority and deep empathy.

A portfolio backed by data and results — the exact copy variants you tested, the structural or psychological changes you made, and the quantifiable lift they produced in metrics like CTR, quiz completion, or conversion rates.

Deep lifecycle marketing mastery: significant experience building and optimizing automated email and SMS flows (onboarding, retention, win-backs), rather than just writing one-off promotional campaigns. You know how to nurture a lead over time.

A strong foundation in A/B testing mechanics — comfortable formulating clear copy hypotheses, understanding sample size requirements, and knowing when a test result has achieved true statistical significance.

The balance of empathy and compliance: a proven ability to write about highly sensitive personal health topics (menopause symptoms, hormonal changes, wellness) with profound empathy while strictly adhering to healthcare compliance rules and ad-network medical restrictions.

An experimenter’s ego — you don’t take copy critiques personally. You’re obsessed with letting consumer behavior and performance data crown the winning angle.

As a full-time member of our team, you’ll enjoy:

Paid non-working holidays per country of residence

Medical/ Dental/ Vision insurance (US-W2)

Employee engagement activities and virtual gatherings

#7169. Boston University misses revenue projections by $42M

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The private institution will have to change “on a much broader scale” rather than relying on one-time cuts, its CFO said Monday.

BU has already gone through painful cuts as it has looked to shrink expenses. Last year, officials announced plans to lay off 120 staff members while eliminating another 120 vacancies. The cuts were an effort to shave 5% from the university’s budget during the previous fiscal year.

“Our prior cuts were effective in giving us time for thoughtful transformation,” Tirella said Monday.

But officials say those sorts of measures aren’t enough to fix today’s issues. Instead, BU is looking at making “better use” of its facilities, standardizing some operations and reducing duplicating functions, said Chris Sedore, BU’s chief transformation officer.

Manuel Cuevas-Trisán, BU’s senior vice president who oversees HR, suggested the university could upskill and promote employees as it tries to make the most of its resources. “Each vacancy can become an opportunity to think about how the work is done,” he said.

BU officials are also mulling the institution’s offerings. Sedore said some recently launched master’s programs are performing well while others face low enrollment.

The revenue miss for fiscal 2027 follows challenges from the previous year. Last October, BU President Melissa Gilliam and other senior leaders announced a $30 million budget shortfall amid missed revenue and lower-than-expected graduate student enrollment. Leaders at the time sought to address the gap with one-time savings, which Tirella said are no longer enough to address the university’s fiscal challenges.

Those efforts helped shore up the budget overall. With both tuition and grant revenue sliding, BU’s overall fiscal 2026 revenue fell 1.4% year over year to $2.7 billion, but it preserved an operating surplus, according to its fiscal latest financials. However, that net surplus plummeted by 91.6% year over year to $4 million.

Despite fiscal challenges, BU has planned salary increases for employees this fiscal year, as well as spending on deferred maintenance and technology, officials said.

Tirella said BU’s endowment and reserves are providing the university a stable financial foundation. But they cannot fully address operating budget challenges, given restrictions on most endowment funds and fixed spending limits to maintain the endowment’s health over the long run.

“Our balance sheet, including cash reserves and the endowment, cannot sustainably offset ongoing declines in operating margins,” she said.

#7165. Senior Forward Deployed Engineer at Clutch

Clutch builds AI agents that work on behalf of credit unions. Emma, our collections agent, is live at five credit unions and handled 60,000+ member conversations in her first twelve weeks at one of them. Behind her is a growing portfolio: origination, fraud step-up, member (re)engagement and activation agents, all built on the same Agents Platform (TypeScript, NestJS, our own agent SDK, MCP, Temporal, promptfoo).

As a FDE you are one of the two engineers on an agent. You take it from "sold" to "live and performing" at a specific credit union: you sit in the discovery sessions, you build the integrations to their core banking and loan systems, you author and tune the agent's behavior, you run the live tests with their staff, and you stay with it through go-live and the first weeks of production. When something needs a person in the room, you are the one who flies.

This is a senior engineering role, not a solutions or support role. You write production code in our monorepo every week: integrations, modules, and the agents themselves (prompts, tools, evals), together with the other engineers on the pod. The difference from a traditional engineer is that you are on site more often, you make judgment calls in front of the customer, and you own the outcome. What you learn in the field flows straight back into the product: defaults, playbooks, and the platform capabilities the next agent will need.

You build; our AI Operations Managers deliver. The pod (pod lead plus engineers) designs and builds each agent in a high-touch way with the first customers. Once the agent is defined and live, AI Ops coordinates the implementations, configuration and ongoing success at the next credit unions. You hand them something that works and a playbook to run it.

This role requires roughly 20% of travel on average, with one- to two-week stretches around kickoffs and go-lives. Based in the US, able to travel on short notice

You'll join the Agents team, a small, senior group operating as a startup inside Clutch: seven engineers, a dedicated AI product group, and AI sales specialists, working directly with some of the largest credit unions in the country. Every agent is staffed by a pair of engineers, one leaning toward the conversation and LLM side, one toward software and integrations, with a pod lead who owns the product day to day. There is no layer between you and the customer, and no layer between you and the platform team.

The operating style is direct, written, and evidence-driven: BLUF updates, evals over opinions, business outcomes over ticket counts. The team is spread across Brazil, Argentina and the US and works async by default. Being on site is a tool we use at specific moments (kickoff, live testing, go-live), not a permanent posting.

Ship your first production change to a live agent through the team's review process, and be able to explain how a member's call flows through the Agents Platform end to end

Learn one agent deeply: system prompt, tool contracts, orchestration triggers, compliance gates, and the implementation process from kickoff to go-live

Join at least one credit union implementation as the second engineer, including a live testing session with their staff

Map where the current implementation process loses time and where a default or a tool would have removed a round trip

Own the technical side of one credit union implementation end to end: core and LOS integration, agent configuration, testing, go-live, and the first weeks of production

Run the eval and tuning loop for your agent (golden transcripts, rubric judges, regression suite) and be trusted to change its behavior without a second engineer checking your work

Have completed at least two on-site visits and turned what you heard there into product decisions with your pod lead and PM

Contribute at least one reusable capability back to the Agents Platform that the next implementation will not have to rebuild

Have taken one net-new agent from sold to live at its first credit union, with data you can show the partner

Be the engineer credit union staff ask for by name when something in their deployment needs to change

Have written the implementation playbook for your agent well enough that the AI Ops team runs the second and third credit union without you

Be shaping what the next agents look like, because you have seen where the current ones break in the field

7+ years of software engineering in a fast-paced environment, preferably in SaaS and/or Fintech, with senior TypeScript and Node.js backend experience in a large codebase (NestJS, CQRS, schema-validated boundaries, dependency injection). We do not count years of AI experience; the field is too young for that to mean anything. We look at what you have shipped and how you reason about it

Integration and API engineering: secure service-to-service endpoints, third-party APIs, webhooks, and the patience to work with legacy banking systems that were not designed to be integrated

Security and auth judgment: OAuth and machine-to-machine auth, tenant-scoped access, secrets handling, no PII in logs. Our agents touch sensitive member data and operate under consumer-contact regulation

Customer presence: you can sit across from a VP of Collections, hear a request, and say "here is what I recommend and why", including when the answer is no. You are comfortable making calls in the room and owning them afterwards

Code-quality judgment under deadline pressure: you take on debt deliberately, name it, and pay it down, rather than gold-plating or leaving silent debt behind

Genuine appetite for LLM agents: eval discipline for non-deterministic systems, reading a system prompt like code, understanding that some decisions must never reach the model. Curiosity plus shipped work is enough; research depth is not required

Nice to have, explicitly not required: voice and telephony (LiveKit, Twilio), Temporal or event-driven orchestration, Python, core banking or LOS integration experience, credit union or banking domain knowledge

#7162. Alvernia University to acquire Marian University

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The two Catholic institutions, which are hundreds of miles apart, said the merger would be based on shared values rather than financial necessity.

Alvernia said neither university is entering the merger due to financial trouble.

“Both institutions are approaching this opportunity from a position of strength, guided by a shared commitment to mission alignment, long-term stewardship and expanded opportunities for students,” Alvernia said in its FAQ.

But when compared on financial strength, the two universities are in different weight classes.

Alvernia has posted multimillion budget surpluses over the past decade, outside of a reduced surplus in fiscal 2020 and a small deficit in fiscal 2024. In contrast, Marian’s finances have fluctuated more, and it has run a deficit since fiscal 2023.

The difference likely comes down to enrollment, as it does at most tuition-dependent colleges.

Alvernia had 2,796 students in fall 2024, a 2.4% increase from five years prior, according to federal data. Marian’s student body, meanwhile, declined 24.8% to 1,246 over the same period.

In 2023, Marian leadership declared an enrollment emergency and enacted a round of cuts, eliminating over 20 academic programs and laying off an undisclosed number of staff.

Marian Board Chair Holly Brenner said Monday that the merger with Alvernia will allow Marian to preserve its legacy and create new opportunities.

“Throughout this process, protecting Marian’s mission and identity has been at the heart of our discussions,” Brenner said in a statement. “We believe this proposed path forward with Alvernia offers an opportunity to build on Marian’s strong foundation, create new possibilities for our students, and carry our mission forward into the future.”

In turn, Michael Duff, chair of Alvernia’s board, called the acquisition “a thoughtful, mission-aligned step” for the university, one that “reflects Alvernia’s strength, its disciplined stewardship and its commitment to pursuing opportunities that are consistent with our mission.”

The proposed merger is pending regulatory and accreditor approval, as well as formal sign-off from the universities’ boards and affiliated religious orders. Both institutions said they will seek input from faculty and campus groups as details are hammered out.

#7160. Staff GTM Systems Engineer at LiveKit

LiveKit is building the infrastructure layer for the voice-driven era of computing. Our platform gives developers everything they need to build, test, deploy, scale, and observe agents in production. Founded in 2021, LiveKit powers voice AI applications for OpenAI, xAI, Salesforce, Coursera, Spotify, and thousands of others, collectively facilitating billions of calls each year.

The Staff GTM Systems Engineer at LiveKit is the technical owner and architect of the systems that power our go-to-market engine. You'll decide how our GTM stack is designed, what we build versus buy, and where we invest next — and you'll be hands-on building it: Salesforce architecture, integrations, enrichment pipelines, and the agentic workflows that help Sales and Marketing move faster and smarter.

Salesforce is our system of record for revenue, pipeline, and customer data. HubSpot is our marketing layer for campaigns and lead generation, and what it captures needs to flow cleanly and reliably into Salesforce. Around them sits a growing stack of enrichment, sales intelligence, billing, and data platform tools. You'll own the architecture that ties it all together: disciplined change management, observable pipelines, data models built for AI consumption, and agentic workflows that act on GTM signals at scale — designed to serve the business for years, not quarters.

This role sits at the intersection of GTM, Data, Finance, and Engineering. You'll work directly with senior leaders to shape the GTM systems roadmap, turn business strategy into technical plans, and guide decisions that span multiple teams. You'll also help grow the engineers around you, setting the technical standards and practices the team builds on.

You understand the difference between owning a system and gatekeeping one. Sales lives in Salesforce. Marketing lives in HubSpot. Your job is to make sure both are governed well enough that others can work in them effectively, and powerful enough that the GTM team can do things they couldn't do before.

You'll thrive as a Staff GTM Systems Engineer if you:

Are known as the go-to person for tough technical problems, and have good judgment about which ones matter most.

Think in systems and long time horizons, and can make confident architectural decisions with incomplete information.

Can clearly explain complex technical concepts and tradeoffs to engineers and executives alike.

Believe systems should be managed with engineering discipline.

Ship fast and reliably — your work holds up in production.

Are a fast learner, frequently picking up new languages and tools.

Find real satisfaction in mentoring other engineers and helping them grow.

Own the architecture of the GTM systems stack: define how Salesforce, HubSpot, enrichment, sales intelligence, billing, and the data platform fit together, and write the technical strategy that guides how it evolves.

Lead build-vs-buy and procurement decisions: evaluate vendors, run proofs of concept, assess security and integration fit, and partner with Director of GTM Systems and Analytics, Finance, Legal, and IT on selection and renewals.

Shape the GTM systems roadmap with senior leaders across Sales, Marketing, RevOps, Finance, and Engineering: align on requirements, sequence investments, make clear tradeoffs, and lead cross-functional initiatives to completion.

Own Salesforce as the engineering system of record for revenue: data model, flows and automation, validation rules, field governance, and access control.

Own HubSpot for campaigns and lead generation, along with the HubSpot-Salesforce sync — so leads, contacts, and engagement signals reach the revenue pipeline without drift or silent failures.

Build and maintain integrations across the GTM stack, and partner with GTM Analytics Engineering on reliable data flows to the central data platform.

Design and deploy agentic workflows that consume GTM data, surface actionable signals, and automate manual operations — and set the patterns and guardrails others build on.

Establish observability and change management for critical GTM systems: alerts, dashboards, and runbooks; reviewed code changes, documented config, and rollback paths.

Mentor engineers across experience levels and set the technical standards for the team.

#7156. Penn State wrestles with $124M budget hole

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The public institution projects a $87 million year-over-year drop in tuition revenue amid a fall enrollment decline.

Just over 70% of Penn State’s budget hole is due to falling tuition and fee revenue.

Behind the revenue miss is a fall headcount that is 3,058 students short of the comparable 2025 number. Breaking that down, Penn State said Monday that it enrolled 742 fewer graduate students, 922 fewer international students and 2,384 fewer out-of-state students across its campuses. All three of those groups can have an outsized impact on tuition revenue at a college.

About half of the enrollment decline came at seven regional campuses that are set to close — and had been expected. In explaining the declines, the university also pointed to nationwide trends, including widespread decreases in graduate enrollment and international students.

At the same time, Penn State logged its largest-ever undergraduate class of 43,010 students and a record class of 9,907 first-time, full-time students.

“Even with these challenges, Penn State's market position is strong,” said Matt Melvin, Penn State vice president for enrollment management, in a statement.

Health insurance costs made up the remainder of the university’s fiscal 2027 shortfall in its $2.2 billion education and general budget. By August, healthcare claims had grown 15.7% year over year, the university said Monday. Budgetwise, that led to a $37 million jump above projections.

On an FAQ page about its budget, Penn State said it had no plans for broad cuts to its programs or student services, or for changes in its hiring processes, as it looks to tighten spending. The institution also said it would hold tuition and fee prices steady despite the revenue dip.

The university does, however, already have plans to nix some 47 undergraduate programs after a multiyear review of its offerings. Collectively, those programs enroll about 1.1% of Penn State’s undergraduates. The institution listed another 50 programs as “strategic growth opportunities.”

Penn State’s plan to close seven of its 19 regional Commonwealth campuses is primarily an effort to shore up the institution’s budget. Keeping those locations open would cost the university $40 million annually in financial support and overhead expenses and require a $200 million investment in facilities, according to a 2025 report recommending the closures.

#7147. Majority of students report misusing tech during school hours

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

A Fordham Institute report suggests stricter bans are needed for cellphones and other personal devices, as many students still use technology to cheat.

The findings from Fordham come as the nation's largest school districts this fall announced restrictions around AI use.

In September, Los Angeles Unified School District, the nation's largest school district, announced it would restrict access to AI platforms for all students during the 2026-27 school year. The restrictions will be in place as the district reviews instructional use and safeguards for the technology, according to the district's announcement.

New York City Public Schools also announced in September it would pause AI use for elementary and middle school students.

“Though we might quibble about the details, we at Fordham agree with the impulse,” wrote Brian Fitzpatrick, national research manager at Fordham and the report’s author.

"Taken together, our results suggest that American high school students are caught up in — and often contribute to — a disturbing and interconnected set of problems: rampant distraction, widespread AI misuse, heavy dependence on screens, and grading practices that make it easier to earn high marks without mastering the material," said Fitzpatrick in the analysis.

The report was based on a nationally representative survey of over 2,000 current students and recent graduates, fielded between February and March 2026.

It found that over 50% of students use technology during the school day for social media, and just under that percentage use it to message friends. A third of students said they used it for both recreational games and YouTube viewing unrelated to assignments.

"A key reason that illicit tech use is so pervasive is that school-issued devices provide direct access to the platforms driving it," the report said. For example, two-thirds of respondents said YouTube is accessible on school-issued devices.

The report also found students were least likely to use social media when phones were fully banned, such as keeping them in a locked pouch or at the central office. Students were more likely to use social media if they were allowed access to their phones in hallways and at lunch time or if their phones were only restricted during class times.

That finding echoes another report published by the Brookings Institution in September, which found that cellphone ban enforcement tends to wane over the course of the school year as teacher enforcement wanes overtime. Bell-to-bell bans, the report found, may be more effective and durable overtime.

However, some have pushed back against all-day cellphone bans due to safety concerns for students, who cite school shootings and other emergencies as reasons to keep phones closer.

Every interview is held face to face over Microsoft Teams with a member of our team on camera. If we interview you, we will tell you whether a hiring decision has been made for this role within 45 days of your last interview.

How we use artificial intelligence in hiring

We use AI in one place only. Our hiring team may use Microsoft Copilot to transcribe interviews and draft interview notes, so that interviewers can concentrate on the conversation instead of typing. You will always know before any recording or AI note-taking begins, and you can ask us to take notes manually instead — it will not affect your application.

We do not use AI to screen, assess, rank or select applicants, and no automated system makes a hiring decision at any stage. Every shortlisting, interview and offer decision is made by people on our hiring team.

Equal opportunity, inclusion and accessibility

Diversity and inclusion have always been at the core of our values at AudienceView. A diverse workforce with wide perspectives and creative ideas benefits our clients, the communities where we operate, and all of us as colleagues. We welcome applications from qualified people of every background.

We are committed to an accessible hiring process and provide accommodations and adjustments to applicants with disabilities on request, at every stage of recruitment and employment.

AudienceView does not offer employment to anyone without first ensuring that qualified candidates speak directly with a member of the hiring team. All qualifying conversations take place face to face over Microsoft Teams. We do not make offers of employment without meeting candidates, we never make an offer by text message, and we never ask candidates to pay a fee. If you are asked for personal or financial information by text, or before you have met a member of our hiring team, please disregard it.

If this role sounds like your kind of show and your experience lines up with what we’re looking for, we’d love to hear from you. We’re grateful for every application we receive; however, only qualified candidates selected to move forward in our hiring process will be contacted.

No forms. Just smart, optimized applications.

Remember every detail and next step, without a meeting bot.

Every hiring season, school districts ask the same question: Where are the teachers? We stopped asking and started building.

#7141. Broward schools ‘no longer the only game in town’ as enrollment drops

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The Florida district is doubling down on student recruitment and retention by stepping up marketing and engagement efforts.

This is the second installment of a three-part series on the impact of private school choice on public school enrollment.

When Valerie Wanza started as a teacher in Florida's Broward County Public Schools in 1992, the district was at the beginning of what would be explosive growth in student population over the next decade.

Throughout the 1990s and early 2000s, Broward County Public Schools saw such an increase in enrollment that by 2004, more than 100 of its 315 schools were deemed "critically overcrowded."

Now, 34 years later as the district's chief strategy and innovation officer, Wanza is part of a team of administrators working to turn around an enrollment decline that began in the 2015-16 school year. The student population plummeted by 17.5% over the next decade and is forecast to continue decreasing through at least the 2030-31 school year, according to district data.

The district attributed the drop to various factors: lower birth rates leading to fewer children entering schools, rising housing costs that make it harder for families to live in the desirable area bordering the Atlantic Ocean, fewer immigrant students due to shifts in federal policies, and growing competition from private school choice options.

And with the area's school choice proliferation, Wanza said, Broward County Public Schools is "no longer the only game in town."

Now, to stay financially healthy, the district is taking a comprehensive approach that includes consolidating and closing some school campuses, cutting at least 1,000 staff positions, and implementing other measures to save money. It's also attempting to boost enrollment by vigorously recruiting new students through media campaigns, personalized phone calls, and hosting events to drum up interest in and excitement for the district's schools.

Broward is even turning to an outside vendor for help. Earlier this year, the district approved a one-year contract with student recruitment firm Caissa K12 to help connect with families who have left its public schools, determine their reasons for doing so, attempt to resolve those concerns and inform them about the district's benefits and schooling options.

The performance-based contract said Broward County Public Schools will pay Caissa K12 $850 per recruited student who enrolls in a district school, up to 176 students or a maximum payment of $150,000.

“If we aren’t real about, No. 1, recognizing what adjustments we need to make, and then be bold enough to make them, we’re going to become the Kmart … of the public schools,” Wanza said, referring to the once-dominant discount retailer that filed for bankruptcy in 2002 amid growing competition and other pressures.

The enrollment decline that Broward County Public Schools is experiencing mirrors the challenges faced by many other school districts across the country.

While falling birth rates and rising school choice are often to blame, the multitude of factors can be complex and specific to each region's ability to draw new residents with young families. Nationally, enrollment in traditional public schools is expected to drop from 54.7 million in 2021 to 49.4 million in 2035, according to a 2025 analysis from the Brookings Institution based on U.S. Census and other federal data.

On the other hand, some states are predicted to see increases in public school student populations between fall 2022 and fall 2031. Among those are Idaho (+11%), North Dakota (+7.4%) and Tennessee (+2%), according to data from the U.S. Department of Education.

Enrollment in Florida's public schools is projected to increase by 3% over the same time period.

From 2015-16 through 2025-26, Broward County Public Schools lost 17.5% of its student population, or 38,640 students. That translated to a loss of $352 million in per-pupil revenue over that 11-year span. In fiscal year 2026, which ended June 30, the district expected to show an $80 million shortfall because of continued enrollment loss.

The district has already closed seven schools since the 2025-26 school year and is exploring other closures, consolidations and grade configurations for the future. New data released this fall shows district enrollment dropped by another 10,272 for the 2026-27 school year.

Even so, with 315 schools educating 224,324 students, including at charter schools, Broward County Public Schools is still the 6th largest district in the U.S., according to its website.

Broward leaders are carefully analyzing data that can explain why families aren't enrolling their students in county schools. Joseph Beck, the district's director of demographics and enrollment planning, said his team is constantly coming up with ways to look at the data differently to better understand why students are coming or going.

For instance, the data shows that about 20,000 students, on average, withdraw from Broward County Public Schools annually. That number, which doesn't include graduating seniors, has been consistent over many years, Beck said.

The district also investigates how many new students enroll each year, a figure that can be affected by immigration policy changes. For instance, the district had 3,400 fewer newcomer students in 2025-26 than it did the previous year, Beck said.

The data also shows that 1,700 students enrolled in the 2024-25 school year transferred to a private school the following year using a Florida Family Empowerment Scholarship to help pay tuition.

At the state level, about 561,000 students participated in taxpayer-funded scholarship programs during the 2025-26 school year, according to a Florida Senate analysis. That same year, the state's public schools enrolled about 2.8 million students, state data shows.

Across Broward County, some 35,000 students participated in one of Florida's taxpayer-supported private school choice programs in the 2025-26 school year.

Beck said the data further shows that many of the county's private school choice participants weren't previously enrolled in the district's public schools. "They just completely bypass us altogether," Beck said.

He and other Broward County Public School leaders are aiming to reverse that.

Farrah Wilson, Broward County Public Schools' director of marketing and strategic communications, rapidly ticks off the efforts being made to court new students and their families. There are mailers, social media campaigns, TV ads, school tours, home visits and more.

As public schools across the country get more intentional and strategic about recruitment, they are taking a playbook from private schools. That includes hosting school tours, having "shadow days" for prospective students, and sending mailers to families encouraging them to add public schools to their list of options.

We have the best programs. We have the best teachers. We have the best facilities, athletics. We offer a comprehensive experience that many of our charter and private schools can't offer.

Broward County Public Schools' director of marketing and strategic communications

Broward County is home to 245 private schools serving about 54,000 students, or about 18% of students in the county, according to Private School Review, a site that provides information on K-12 private schools for families.

But competition for students can also come from nearby public school systems.

In a mailer that Broward sent out in mid-July, it targeted families living across the district's borders to the north in Palm Beach County and to the south in Miami-Dade County.

"This is just a way to let parents know that there are options, and if they are interested, they can go to one of our schools on the county line and participate and take one of the open seats," Wilson said.

The mailers led to one student from Palm Beach County and 17 students from Miami-Dade County enrolling in Broward County Public Schools, the district said.

While it might seem unfair to try to poach other districts' students, Broward administrators said it goes both ways. The School District of Palm Beach County has likewise attempted to recruit Broward students living near its border, Broward officials said.

The Palm Beach system shares information about its schools through various channels, and marketing reaches families across the region, including those in neighboring counties like Broward, said Sean Cooley, chief of communications and enrollment for the Palm Beach district, in an Oct. 29 email. However, Palm Beach does not track how many Broward residents enroll in its schools.

"Many families living near county boundaries commute to work within Palm Beach County, making our high-performing schools an attractive and convenient option for their children's education," Cooley said.

Florida has an open boundary policy that lets families enroll in schools outside of their home school district as long as there are open seats available. "We're used to having open lines, if you will. Now we just got more intentional about it," said Wanza.

Broward is also expanding its school choice options by catering to what families want. The district has an online school as well as 32 schools offering dual language programs. It has magnet schools, a high school that operates on a 4-day-a-week schedule, several K-8 schools, a grade 6-12 school and a K-12 school.

In another enrollment initiative, the district is encouraging and supporting each school to tell its own stories through various marketing and communications efforts. For example, on "testimonial Tuesdays," students, families and staff share on social media why their school is great.

Initiatives like this enable families to make connections with individual schools, not necessarily the district as a whole, Wilson said. Building excitement at individual schools is "the secret sauce" to boosting enrollments, she said.

Every school also has an "Enroll Now" button on its website. If a family clicks the link and shares their contact information, the school's principal is automatically notified and is encouraged to reach out directly to the family, answer any questions they have, offer a tour and make other recruiting efforts that will "close the deal," Wilson said.

One reason Broward is taking such an intentional approach to marketing, said administrators, is to combat misinformation or outdated information about district schools, including about its academic performance. Broward County Public Schools has earned an "A" rating from the Florida Department of Education for three consecutive years and continues to "demonstrate strong academic growth," the district said in an email to K-12 Dive.

Negative perceptions of the public school system are what the district is "always battling against," Wilson said. This is why the district is heavily investing in marketing and recruiting, she said.

"We have the best programs. We have the best teachers. We have the best facilities, athletics. We offer a comprehensive experience that many of our charter and private schools can't offer.”

News Graphic Developer Jasmine Ye Han contributed to this story.

Picture a group of middle school math teachers meeting to mull over a specific problem. Their eighth graders keep missing the same assessment question about graphing a system equation, and the educators’ assignment is to figure out why.

#7139. 3 ways public schools are competing for enrollment

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

School leaders are borrowing pages from marketing and advertising executives to develop tactics that will rightsize their enrollment.

This is the third installment of a three-part series on the impact of private school choice on public school enrollment.

The decision to close 129-year-old Perry Elementary School in Belvidere, Ill., earlier this year devastated many community members who didn't want to see their historic building shuttered.

While scores of schools across the country are closing as enrollment declines empty their classrooms, that was not the case with Perry. The 174-student school, in the end, just got old. Its aging structure needed a prohibitive $14 million to $16 million in repairs, according to Cassandra Schug, superintendent of Belvidere Community Unit School District 100.

The district's enrollment has in fact remained relatively stable, with only a modest decline in recent years, Schug says. Enrollment stands at 7,348 students in 2026, down from 7,470 in 2021.

Still, the decision to close Perry and the reaction it engendered from the community offer constructive guidance for administrators everywhere who are staring down school closures brought on by shrinking student populations.

When Perry's closure became inevitable after a 6-1 school board vote in April, the community rallied to usher its beloved school out in style, Schug says. Longtime teacher Susan Thomas spearheaded a committee to honor the school’s legacy with a citywide celebration ultimately attended by hundreds of people in May.

Thomas, who taught 5th grade at Perry for 19 years, says the neighborhood school was “so much more” than just a building. The closure decision, she says, was nothing short of traumatic.

Perry “has been a cornerstone for Belvidere. It needed to be celebrated. It needed us to be able to show our appreciation for what it had meant for so many years, and I just wanted it to be done right,” Thomas says.

The celebration allowed people across the community to visit and view old yearbooks, photos, letters, newspaper articles and student projects — all organized by decade and displayed on tables and walls throughout the gym.

“So you could walk through and just see all of that Perry history and recognize how education had changed over the years, how the building had changed over the years, and again, just really spend time sort of saying goodbye,” Schug says.

The celebration “helped us all to process what had been a really difficult time," honor the school's legacy, "create that closure, and give us a chance to celebrate those positive memories that Perry had brought to the community.”

Beginning this school year, Perry's staff members and students dispersed to other schools in the district, with students transferring through a lottery system.

Though Belvidere enrollment is expected to stay steady, district officials are carefully watching enrollment numbers and projections, especially as more public schools nationwide experience declines, Schug says.

And, she adds, she hopes she never has to close another school in her career again.

As some school communities grieve shuttered schools, districts are proactively working to boost enrollments and stave off closures. Here are three examples of how school leaders are striving to stay competitive.

The days of traditional public school systems having a monopoly on education and just worrying about teaching students are gone, says Brian Stephens, CEO of Caissa K12, a Memphis-based strategic communications firm.

In 2009, Stephens co-founded a public strategy firm that specialized in complex communication crisis management. But in 2017, as he saw more states adopt private school choice programs, the firm converted to focusing exclusively on student recruitment and retention for traditional public schools.

Currently, Caissa K12 — named for the mythical goddess of chess and strategy — works with nearly 100 districts in 26 states. The company says it has helped recruit about 55,000 students for its clients through research, communications, events and knocking on doors.

The firm is one of a growing number of players in this space.

Taking a page from the retail industry, one of Caissa K12's services for its district clients is performing "secret shopper" outreach. Caissa K12 employees or contractors act as families interested in enrolling their child and visit or connect with public and private schools to look for enrollment or communication barriers or other customer service problems.

The company works with school districts including Florida's Broward County Public Schools through performance contracts, meaning Caissa K12 only gets paid if the company meets an agreed-upon student recruitment goal.

When it comes to recruiting and retaining students, Stephens says, the greatest mistakes public schools make are not advocating for themselves and not being willing to improve flaws like hard-to-navigate websites.

Those steps may sound easy, but they are difficult for school systems with a lot on their plates, he says.

If the government and the people want public schools to "act like a business instead of a public good, then we have to act more like a business," Stephens says. "That means we need to advocate for ourselves."

Districts that are innovative and want to build trust with families "absolutely have to reach out and let people know that they exist and let people know how awesome they are," he says.

Stephens points to creative approaches public schools can take to help attract and keep students.

One district client, he notes, installed a washer and dryer at a school to support school attendance by making sure children could have clean clothes. Other schools have provided feminine hygiene products, which also boosted school attendance, Stephens says.

Eliminating outdated and abrasive policies can make a difference too. For instance, schools need to remove attendance policies that punish students for having overdue library books or unpaid fees for band uniforms, he says.

“Stepping up those things has really helped. You have to identify what the issue is that's stopping a child from attending.”

A new federal school choice program launching next year has many public school communities wondering how their students might benefit from billions of dollars in expected donations for K-12 educational expenses.

Nevada's Clark County School District, for example, is considering a collaboration with its Public Education Foundation, which would serve as a scholarship-granting organization to direct donations to benefit the Las Vegas-area district's students. Those extra scholarship funds could be targeted toward career-connected learning opportunities, technology, tutoring and more, according to an Aug. 13 presentation to the district's board of trustees.

Elsewhere, public school advocates from Iowa to Denver are also making plans to direct some of these taxpayer contributions to public schools.

And at least one new national scholarship-granting organization has formed to funnel donations to services supporting public school students. The Future School Fund, founded by Deborah Gist, former Rhode Island commissioner of education and state education superintendent in the District of Columbia, intends to help schools identify programs where scholarship funds can be used.

The federal Education Freedom Tax Credit program, which launches on Jan. 1, will guide taxpayer donations to students attending either public or private schools, who can then use the scholarships to pay for allowable educational expenses.

This first-ever nationwide school choice program is projected to raise nearly $26 billion annually for about 2.2 million scholarships by 2030, according to the administering agencies, the U.S. Department of the Treasury and the IRS.

This could overshadow the nation's current largest education program, Title I, which has an $18.4 billion budget for fiscal year 2026 budget to aid children at low-income schools. Title I serves about 26 million students in 90% of the country's school districts, according to the Bipartisan Policy Center.

Only students living in states that opt into the Education Freedom Tax Credit program would be eligible to receive scholarships. As of Oct. 22, 30 states have indicated that they will opt into the program, according to the IRS.

Participating states will approve scholarship-granting organizations to operate in their states, which will then direct taxpayer donations to families to use for education expenses.

ACE Scholarships is one such SGO. The Denver-based nonprofit is working with public and private school systems in several states interested in soliciting donations for the federal tax credit scholarships that can then be used at their schools, according to CEO Norton Rainey.

ACE Scholarships already has several charter school clients and is in talks with public school districts to manage and distribute their donation accounts, he says. The organization, he adds, will focus on directing scholarships to students with the greatest financial needs.

But while some are urging public school systems and supporters to embrace the federal scholarship program, critics have many concerns. They worry about a lack of accountability, a financial drain on public schools and potential inequities.

"Simply permitting some public school students to benefit does not ensure that resources are equitably distributed or that schools and students most in need are served by the program," according to a coalition of 16 civil rights advocacy organizations in a Sept. 28 letter to state governors.

The rapid increase in school choice options is leading more public school leaders to consider creative ways to invest in marketing to keep their enrollment numbers up, says Mellissa Braham, associate director of the National School Public Relations Association.

But that doesn’t mean they necessarily have the budgets to do so, Braham says.

“Because districts nationwide are facing really tough times right now, a lot of them are having to reduce staff, and in that light, it's painful to put additional focus on marketing, even though ultimately it does help your budget,” she says.

One way districts are approaching enrollment marketing in a small yet impactful way is by having dedicated in-house staff solicit student and teacher testimonials and create videos to post on their communication channels.

Other low-cost marketing strategies include paying to boost social media posts or have their school or district website appear on search engines more frequently instead of relying on organic search, Braham says. Some districts are paying for targeted marketing emails, too.

She points to successful elements she's seen in school marketing campaigns, including firsthand testimonials from students and families, a focus on what makes a school and district unique, and targeted emails that feel specific to the person receiving it.

But districts in highly competitive areas may need to invest in more costly marketing efforts, she says. That could include buying billboard, television or streaming ads.

“If you're in a really competitive environment with a lot of different schools to choose from, it might be an investment you have to make with the ultimate return of enrollment, which brings in funds, which allows you to support staff programs and services,” Braham says.

In districts where school choice options are fairly new, Braham says, sometimes the biggest challenge for school leaders is to understand that families are now customers and that neighborhood public schools are not the only option.

Shifting to a marketing mindset is different from sending out regular school communications, which aim to share information and build relationships, she says. And that change is “new for a lot of public schools.”

#7137. Baylor stares down $30M deficit after undergraduate enrollment dip

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The Baptist institution in Texas is pausing hiring and delaying capital spending as it tries to shore up the budget gap.

Livingstone’s deficit announcement follows a slide in Baylor’s undergraduate enrollment this fall.

Baylor’s total undergraduate headcount fell 2.3% year over year to 13,860 students, according to institutional data. First-time, first-year enrollment dipped 6%, representing a decline of over 200 students, which could bring ongoing financial ramifications given that they might have continued paying tuition for several years.

“Undergraduate enrollment is realigning to historic levels now that the two large COVID-era freshman classes have matriculated through the University,” Livingstone said. The university brought record numbers of students in 2020 and 2021, bucking the national trend during that period.

However, graduate enrollment increased by 3.8% to 5,888 students.

Livingstone rattled off a long list of headwinds that have dogged Baylor’s budget. Among them were rising spending, families wary of college prices, competitors increasingly discounting tuition to recruit students and “increasing volatility in forecasting budgets in an uncertain financial environment.”

Baylor’s leader also acknowledged the sum the university owes the head football coach it just fired after a slow start to the season. The buyout reportedly amounts to an estimated $12 million for Dave Aranda. Livingstone described the costs as a one-time expense not included in the deficit and “a liability of Baylor Athletics that will be addressed solely by the department.”

In the previous fiscal year, the Division I university brought in about $63 million in revenue from college sports, according to its financial statements.

Baylor started the calendar year in cost-cutting mode. In February, Livingstone announced a plan to shave $35 million from the university’s budget over two years, which she framed as necessary to limit tuition increases on students. Among other moves, the university slashed retirement contributions for employees.

In her message Monday, the university president acknowledged the sacrifices Baylor’s workforce has already made to help shore up the budget.

“As faithful Christian stewards, we must work together to prepare Baylor to succeed in a very uncertain future for colleges and universities that is likely to look quite different than the past,” she said.

Baylor joins a handful of others to issue early reports on fiscal 2027 woes amid enrollment pressures.

Just this week, Boston University leaders said the private institution missed revenue estimates by $42 million so far, and Pennsylvania State University revealed a projected $124 million budget hole due primarily to tuition revenue declines. Both institutions plan on budget cuts but did not signal that layoffs would be part of the savings efforts.

#7134. A tale of two families: Why one left Broward schools — and another stayed

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The reasons for coming or going are as varied as families themselves, but learning what drives their decisions can help public schools retain students.

This is a sidebar for the second installment of a three-part series on the impact of private school choice on public school enrollment.

To better understand parents' school choice decisions, we interviewed two Broward County, Florida, families — the Frys and the Gibson-Petersons. While each family has their own reasons for leaving or staying in the district's public schools, learning about these families' experiences may be helpful to the broader school choice discussion.

When Nancy Fry was a student in Florida's Broward County Public Schools in the mid 1990s to early 2000s, a population surge meant that the district was literally bursting out of its buildings. Fry's high school, in fact, had so many students that her entire 9th grade learned in portable classrooms.

Now, as she raises her two children in the same district, she's watching how underenrolled some schools are becoming. As a volunteer on school advisory boards, Fry has a front row seat to the struggles Broward faces as reduced funding is leading to staff layoffs and school closures.

"I saw — from one year to the next — my daughter's elementary school lose almost $4 million in funding just because of enrollment," said Fry, who is also a community organizer for Families for Strong Public Schools, a nonprofit that advocates for high-quality public schools across Florida.

Even with the reduced staffing and funding, Fry said, the public school system provides more learning and extracurricular opportunities for her children than private schools would. Her son is in 9th grade and participates in the international Cambridge program, an academically rigorous curriculum.

Additionally, her son has access to college-level courses, Advanced Placement classes, sports, theater and other activities. Fry's daughter is a 5th grader in the gifted program. A benefit of the public school system is that it must educate all students, regardless of ability, income or other factors, Fry said.

"Public schools are the great equalizer," she said. "Everyone has access to the same type of education, regardless of your income and your ZIP code."

At the same time, Fry said she knows public school is not a perfect fit for everybody.

Still, she said she knows Broward County Public Schools provides a quality education, and added that she's hopeful the district can attract more students and be more financially secure.

"Just having the stability of not worrying about: Is there going to be funding? Is there going to be a state takeover? Is the school going to have to close? Not having to worry about that, I think, would also help draw people back into the public schools," Fry said.

She also said Florida should invest more in its public school systems.

"I hope that the state decides to prioritize our students again and fund our public schools throughout the state accordingly, not just for Broward, but every school district. I believe every school district should get funding to meet the needs of the students, and that we can do better by our kids and make sure that they don't have to struggle to get what they need."

After two years of her daughter being bullied in 2nd and 3rd grade at her Broward County public school, Simone Gibson-Peterson enrolled her daughter in a private Christian school with tuition assistance from Florida's Hope Scholarship program for students who have experienced bullying, harassment, assault and other violations.

The Hope scholarship is now part of the state's universal Family Empowerment Scholarship program.

"I should not have to send my child to school and worry about her coming home feeling intimidated or not wanting to go back to school, and that was my biggest problem. Like, I cried every day having to watch my daughter go back into the same situation that she left the day before," said Gibson-Peterson. The mother said that as a student in Broward County Public Schools in the late 1980s, she herself was bullied and got into fights.

In the 12th grade, Gibson-Peterson dropped out of school, but later earned a GED and continued her education. She eventually earned a master's degree in criminal justice with a concentration in child abuse.

Her daughter, now a 7th grader, is thriving at her new school, Gibson-Peterson said. "There's so much accountability. They have zero tolerance for bullying, like that's not even going to happen," she said.

Her daughter's grades improved, and she is flourishing socially, her mom said. The school provides tutoring and extracurricular activities, as well as extra time for tests for her daughter, who has ADHD. "She's into track, she's into cheerleading, and she's just overall fully involved in school and happy about it."

The $8,000-a-year scholarship doesn't fully meet the $16,000 annual tuition, so Gibson-Peterson and her ex-husband split the expense, which comes to about $560 each a month. But, she said, the tuition covers most costs, including field trips.

Even though Gibson-Peterson is pleased with her family's decision to leave the public school system and enroll in private school, she said that because she works in child protection services, she's a firm supporter of a healthy and safe public school system.

The children she works with "need the public school system, but I also believe in school choice because not all of the schools have great teachers or great staff," Gibson-Peterson said.

"I want to be able to know that when I send my children — not just my biological child but my children that live in my foster care village — I want to know that when I send them to school, that they're safe."

#7131. Colleges report declines in international enrollment amid federal restrictions

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Survey data released Wednesday found that nearly 6 in 10 U.S. colleges lost foreign graduate students this fall, and 45% saw a decline in undergraduates.

Wednesday’s survey, released by NAFSA: Association of International Educators in partnership with the Oxford University Press and the student recruitment platform Studyportals, reinforces concerns that the Trump administration's attacks on foreign students and the long-standing U.S. programs that support their study here would corrode the international enrollment pipeline.

Survey respondents cited the administration’s crackdown on student visas and the astronomical new fees sought for the H-1B visa and Optional Practical Training programs. While some of those policies are being challenged in the courts, they can signal to international students that they are unwelcome in the U.S., and the uncertainty and instability can ward off prospective applicants.

Major public research universities, traditionally popular among foreign students, have begun reporting international enrollment declines this fall. And some, like Pennsylvania State University, have drawn a direct line between that loss of students and budgetary challenges.

“These survey results affirm what we’ve observed before: national policies matter to international students making major decisions about where to study, conduct research, or start a career,” Fanta Aw, NAFSA’s executive director and CEO, said in a statement Wednesday.

Some colleges also reported decreased interest in countries that have historically sent the most higher education students overseas, such as China and India.

“Our agents in India in particular are telling us that they are seeing a drastic decline in inquiries for US graduate study and that many prospective students are either considering other destinations or postponing study plans,” one university respondent said.

Of those surveyed, 37% of U.S. colleges said they planned to diversify the locations where they recruit from and tap into new markets over the next 12 months.

One institution said it would pivot to the Philippines, Canada and Colombia as a means of offsetting losses from India, Bangladesh and Nepal, the three countries from which the college traditionally got the most foreign students.

Another 32% said they would expand their online offerings, and 30% said they would turn to budget cuts.

However, cuts could further exacerbate challenges for colleges’ international admissions offices. One respondent admitted that staffing issues have “resulted in a lack of attention to incoming students.”

Pollsters surveyed colleges’ senior administrators and international student recruitment professionals online between Oct. 5 and 26.
