#7129. Is private school choice a ‘tipping point’ for school closures?

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

As declining birth rates drive enrollment challenges, district leaders and public school advocates worry that school choice programs will further strain budgets.

This is the first installment of a three-part series on the impact of private school choice on public school enrollment.

That's the message families across the country are hearing from both public and private schools as competition for students heats up in the wake of declining birth rates and the rapid growth of taxpayer-supported private school choice. And while private schools are used to marketing their offerings, it's a new world for public schools that historically were the first — and most often only — choice for the great majority of families.

Now school districts large and small are scrambling for students as they watch their enrollments fall. They are boosting academic programs, giving personalized tours, and analyzing data on why students stay or go as they face greater rivalry with private schools. All of this is adding strain to already cash-strapped public schools as government funding is tied to enrollment. Throughout the U.S., public schools are closing or consolidating because they simply don't have enough students to fill their classrooms.

When Pflugerville Independent School District’s board of trustees in May approved closing four elementary schools in the 2027-28 school year, the district’s superintendent said a $20 million budget shortfall made the decision necessary.

The closures are expected to save the Texas district $15 million to $16 million a year as it grapples with declining enrollment alongside rising or fixed costs, said Pflugerville Superintendent Quintin Shepherd. Between the 2019-20 and 2025-26 school years, enrollment fell 5% from 26,400 students to 25,046, according to district data.

And Pflugerville ISD may face more difficult decisions about its budget and school consolidations in the future, district officials fear.

All of these things, we have to respond to simultaneously ... It sort of feels like an avalanche.

Superintendent, Pflugerville Independent School District

The district’s enrollment has dropped for a variety of reasons, Shepherd said, including a decline in birth rates, challenges with local housing affordability, changing demographics, charter school growth, virtual school options, migration patterns, open enrollment — and more recently a $1 billion universal statewide school choice program in Texas that’s rolling out this school year.

“All of these things, we have to respond to simultaneously,” Shepherd said. “It sort of feels like an avalanche.”

The state projected in April that Pflugerville ISD’s enrollment could drop as much as 1.6% — or by 389 students — in the 2026-27 school year directly because of the state's new Texas Education Freedom Accounts. That would amount to a loss of $4.3 million in state funds for the district.

“This is where the voucher conversation becomes so darn important,” Shepherd said.

While not all enrollment declines are driven by vouchers, he said, for those districts "already facing demographic challenges, the vouchers could become a tipping point. And that tipping point is what’s going to trigger the avalanche.”

For Pflugerville ISD specifically, Texas’ new school choice program “could become another tipping point,” Shepherd said.

“I think it puts everything back on the table for us,” he added. “Potential future closures, potential program cuts, and so on and so forth. It’s that our system has got to remain flexible and adaptive to the situation and the context as it continues to evolve.”

As enrollment challenges continue for public school districts nationwide, school closures have steadily increased in recent years.

While 755 schools closed in the 2021-22 school year, the number jumped to 1,011 in the 2024-25 school year, according to the National Center for Education Statistics. That seems to signal a return to school closure trends from before the aberration of COVID-19 pandemic times. For instance, NCES data shows 1,122 closures in 2018-19.

However, the national data on school closures is limiting, said Marguerite Roza, director of the Edunomics Lab and research professor at Georgetown University, as it does not yet capture the spike in school closures over the past two school years. During that time, she said, closures appear to have drastically picked up mostly due to sweeping birthrate declines.

Even where you see a lot of school choice, a lot of the vouchers are going to kids who are already in private schools.

School choice policies may be a variable that is impacting closures in certain communities, Roza said, but they are not the primary factor driving school closures. The key underlying issue is declining birth rates, she said.

“Even where you see a lot of school choice, a lot of the vouchers are going to kids who are already in private schools,” Roza said.

At the same time, some district leaders, public school advocates and researchers worry that large statewide school choice programs like those in Arizona, Florida and now Texas, could make matters worse and speed up public school closures.

Between 2022 and 2031, public school enrollment is expected to fall nationally by 5.5% — or 2.7 million students, according to an April analysis of NCES data by the Education Commission of the States. That enrollment loss would lead to a widespread decline in state revenue for public schools of $11.5 billion a year by the 2030-31 school year, projects a separate report by Bellwether and WestEd.

The ongoing enrollment drops are largely due to decreasing birth rates, the two education nonprofits said. But contributing factors include a “national slowdown in immigration,” families migrating out of state in some places, and growing interest in private school and homeschooling fueled by school choice initiatives.

Private schools, meanwhile, are not immune to the enrollment challenges and pressures from school choice competition. For instance, of 5,829 member schools of the National Catholic Educational Association, 62 closed or consolidated during the 2025-26 school year, and only 13 schools opened, for a net loss of less than 1% of the total number of NCEA member schools.

The competitive pressure public schools are feeling from private school choice programs will likely intensify as the first federal school choice program available nationwide launches next year. The massive and controversial Education Freedom Tax Credit program is expected to fund 2.2 million taxpayer-supported student scholarships totaling $26 billion each year by 2030, according to the IRS, which along with the U.S. Department of Treasury where it is housed, is administering the program.

Only students living in states that opt into the program will be eligible to receive funds through a scholarship-granting organization. According to the IRS, 30 states indicated they will participate as of Oct. 22. The Treasury Department issued temporary and proposed rules for the program Oct. 1.

Opponents consider the program a threat to public school funding that will only benefit wealthy families. Supporters, on the other hand, say that because scholarships can go toward both private school tuition and public school expenses, the program will expand educational opportunities and funding for all students. Covered public school expenses can include tutoring, ed tech and services for students with disabilities.

"Everyone is a winner in the education freedom tax credit, whether it's a private school or it's a public school," said Norton Rainey, CEO of ACE Scholarships, a Denver-based nonprofit scholarship-granting organization.

But Augustus Mays, vice president of partnerships and engagement at EdTrust, said taxpayer funds shouldn't be “funneled” to private schools, which often don't accept all applicants. Instead, federal tax credit dollars should go into public funding streams like Title I and the Individuals with Disabilities Education Act that “support all students,” he said during a forum hosted by Sen. Mazie Hirono, D-Hawaii, on Capitol Hill in Washington, D.C., on Sept. 22.

“This is not free money or a targeted investment in students with the greatest needs,” Mays said. “It is a[n] uncapped federal tax subsidy steering public resources toward private education with few safeguards.”

Meanwhile, public school advocates and researchers have said a majority of state private school choice program funds in Arizona, Florida and Texas have primarily benefited students already enrolled in private school or being homeschooled.

While that may mean there hasn’t been a “mass exodus” of public school students moving to private schools, even small groups transferring out can still “meaningfully impact” public schools — especially when enrollment is already slipping because of declining birth rates, said Joshua Cowen, a professor of education policy at Michigan State University.

When a state obligates more of its budget to expand voucher programs, he said, over time the state won’t be able to keep public school revenue above rising inflation costs.

“That leaves districts on their own at the local level, which leads to closure if you’re underenrolled or you’re just under financial distress," Cowen said. “So there’s like a downstream effect of all this.”

In the 2011-12 school year, Arizona implemented the nation’s oldest education savings account program. The state also became one of the first to expand its private school choice program to include all eligible students, beginning in the 2022-23 school year.

Education savings accounts, or ESAs, are funded from taxpayer dollars and can be used by parents for various educational purposes, including private school tuition, school transportation and tutoring. ESAs differ from vouchers, which give eligible families a portion of taxpayer-supported funding to pay for private school expenses.

Arizona’s school choice program’s participation boomed as more students gained eligibility, jumping from 12,000 students in 2021-22 to 88,000 by 2024-25, according to a Rand Corp. analysis published in November 2025. By the 2024-25 school year, about 7% of students in the state had an education savings account for private school tuition and other educational expenses.

Since 2022, Arizona school districts have shuttered 41 of the state's 2,418 public schools, according to a tracker by Save Our Schools Arizona, a public school advocacy group. Of the closures, 25 — or 61% — were Title I schools serving low-income communities.

We've long said that Arizona should serve as a warning sign for the rest of the nation of what a universal voucher program will do to public education.

Communications manager, Save Our Schools Arizona

One reason schools are closing in Arizona is because districts in the state historically overbuilt schools to the point that about a third of classroom seats are empty, said Katie Ratlief, executive director at Common Sense Institute Arizona, a nonpartisan research organization focused on fiscal policy. Ratlief served in leadership roles in then-Gov. Doug Ducey’s administration at the time when he signed the ESA expansion into law.

According to Ratlief, families were already leaving Arizona public school systems before the state’s ESA program launched. The slow student exodus that began in 2008 “created the political environment where ESAs could pass and move forward in Arizona because parents were demanding them,” she said.

Nonetheless, some public school leaders and advocates in the state have attributed the latest school closures to enrollment declines driven by Arizona's ESA program.

For instance, one official at Arizona’s Kyrene Independent School District partially blamed the expansion of private school choice for a recent decision to close six of its 25 schools starting this school year.

During a board meeting on the initial proposal, Superintendent Laura Toenjes said the district’s declining enrollment was being compounded by rising housing costs and charter school growth. Moreover, she said, the state's ESA funds "unfairly incentivize families to leave traditional public schools.”

Tyler Kowch, communications manager at Save Our Schools Arizona, said the state is a bellwether as school choice policies go national.

“We've long said that Arizona should serve as a warning sign for the rest of the nation of what a universal voucher program will do to public education."

News Graphic Developer Jasmine Ye Han contributed to this story.

#7126. York College nears $7.4M purchase of shuttering Penn State campus

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The private institution intends to turn the 53-acre property into a healthcare education hub through a partnership with a local hospital system.

While Penn State saw the York campus as a financial burden, York College said Thursday it sees the location as an opportunity.

York College President Thomas Burns said in a statement that the private college “is uniquely equipped to preserve this campus as a central educational hub.”

Early planning between York College and WellSpan call for an expansion of nursing and other healthcare education partnerships. Those partnerships include one struck last year between WellSpan and Temple University’s medical school to open a regional campus in York.

York College said it would finance the property acquisition with its endowment — last valued at $193.8 million — while WellSpan would commit “significant financial investment” toward capital improvements on the campus. According to Penn State, the campus had a nearly $30 million maintenance backlog as of last year.

Penn State will continue operating the campus through the spring 2027 semester as previously announced. Afterward, pending approval of the sale, York College will “lead campus operations” while WellSpan will have an “operational and instructional” presence on the campus.

Penn State opted last year to close its York campus as part of a plan to shore up its central budget and refocus on its strongest areas.

At Penn State York, enrollment declined by over 40% to 703 students between 2014 and 2024, according to a university report. Enrollment declined by 15% between 2019 and 2024.

“Despite the efforts of campus leadership and faculty to stabilize enrollment through program enhancements and community outreach, the numbers have not rebounded,” Penn State administrators wrote.

Meanwhile at York College, enrollment slid 15.1% to 3,656 students between 2019 and 2022, according to federal data. But over the next two years that increased to 3,825 students. And the college says it has seen four consecutive years of increases.

In recommending the York campus closure, Penn State administrators cited the ongoing enrollment slide, as well as “regional market saturation” and “high operating costs relative to its size and scale.”

On the last point, they pointed to challenges related to scale. Its small campus, which has 15 buildings but no student housing, made it difficult to attract students outside the commuter region.

“While its facilities are functional, the campus lacks the infrastructure or strategic location necessary to serve as a long-term hub,” the leaders wrote in their 2025 report. “As enrollment has fallen, per-student operating costs have increased, and the campus has become increasingly dependent on central resources to remain operational.”

As for regional competition, York College is just a mile away, as is a community college. Penn State’s own Harrisburg campus is 17 miles away, presenting internal competition. Altogether, there were 19 colleges within 30 miles of Penn State York, according to the university’s report.

Prior to York College’s offer, local officials had asked Penn State to donate the campus for a local arts and workforce training center.

Renata Engel, a Penn State vice president who oversees the commonwealth campuses, said in a statement Thursday, “The community planning process identified continued educational use as a priority, and this proposal would place the campus with an established local institution while also serving the University’s financial interests.”

#7123. Trump taps DEI opponent to lead DOJ’s civil rights division

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

While at the U.S. Department of Education, Craig Trainor threatened to strip federal funding from colleges over their diversity initiatives.

Trainor’s selection signals that the Trump administration isn’t backing away from its heavy crackdown on colleges and their diversity initiatives. Trainor is set to replace Harmeet Dhillon, one of several top DOJ officials who stepped down after Democrats seized control of both chambers of Congress in the midterm elections.

Under Dhillon, the department’s civil rights division aggressively investigated colleges over pro-Palestinian protests and their policies for admissions, leading several high-profile colleges to cut deals with the Trump administration to end those probes.

The DOJ has even sued the University of California system, alleging that its Los Angeles campus hasn’t adequately responded to harassment toward Jewish and Israeli students.

The agency also sued Harvard University in February to force the Ivy League institution to hand over extensive admissions data and again in March to cut off its access to federal student aid. Judges dismissed both cases against Harvard, but the agency has since appealed the federal student aid case to the 1st U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals.

DOJ officials have recently stepped up their crusade against initiatives aimed at recruiting a diverse study body. So far, its investigations into admissions policies have led the department to accuse 10 medical and law schools of racially discriminating against White and Asian applicants.

Trainor has likewise spearheaded major efforts to stamp out DEI in the higher education sector. While serving as the Education Department’s acting assistant secretary for civil rights, Trainor threatened in February 2025 guidance to pull federal funding from colleges over DEI initiatives ranging from race-based scholarships to affinity graduation ceremonies.

Federal courts have since struck down the guidance. However, the DOJ released similar sweeping guidance last year that threatens to strip federal grant funding from colleges over what the agency deems as illegal DEI. DOJ officials flagged several commonplace practices as potentially unlawful, ranging from requiring job applicants to provide diversity statements to having identity-based lounges — even if they are open to all.

Trainor pursued similar anti-DEI policies in his most recent appointment as the U.S. Department of Housing and Urban Development’s assistant secretary for fair housing and equal opportunity. In that role, Trainor issued guidance warning colleges that affinity housing may violate federal law.

#7118. Director of Technology Partnerships at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We’re looking for a Director of Technology Partnerships to lead the teams responsible for growing Supabase through technology integrations and our marketplace ecosystem.

You’ll bring together two complementary motions: partnerships that embed Supabase into the products developers already use, and a marketplace that helps developers discover and connect complementary technologies to their Supabase projects. Your job is to turn both into durable sources of revenue, adoption, and customer value.

Reporting to the Head of Partnerships, you’ll lead and develop the Integrations and Marketplace teams, set their commercial strategy, and work hands-on with our most important partners. A central part of the role is working closely with AI labs to build strategic relationships, develop integrations, and bring joint opportunities to market. You’ll partner closely with Product, Engineering, Sales, Marketing, and Finance to take integrations from idea to launch—and grow their impact long after launch.

This role is based in the San Francisco Bay Area, where you’ll build relationships with leading AI, developer tools, and infrastructure companies. Supabase is remote-first, and you’ll work across a globally distributed team.

#7116. Report: AI's Productivity Gains in Science Tempered by Time Spent Validating Outputs

Artificial intelligence is saving scientists significant amounts of time, but researchers are giving some of those gains back by checking the technology's work, according to a new study involving Google, Google DeepMind, and MIT. The findings add a scientific-workflow dimension to a much broader debate over unreliable, misaligned, and increasingly autonomous AI systems — without establishing that the more dramatic forms of AI misbehavior are occurring in scientific research.

Rather than the current, raging debate about "rogue AI" systems deliberately escaping control, deceiving scientists, or sabotaging experiments, the researchers are documenting something more immediate: AI output that requires auditing and verification, concerns about growing quantities of low-quality research, and a research pipeline in which faster hypothesis generation can run ahead of human and physical capacity to validate the results. The paper cites worries that AI-generated content, hallucinations, and what prior researchers have called illusions of understanding could strain peer review and validation.

The September paper, AI in Science: Early Insights, combines three main empirical sources: approximately 15 million anonymized Gemini interactions, an inventory of 2,690 specialized scientific AI models, and a survey of 637 active scientists in the United States and United Kingdom. Nearly half of the surveyed scientists reported using some form of AI every day, and researchers who reported net time savings averaged just under seven hours a week.

Google separately highlighted the findings in its Sept. 15 AI & Economy ATLAS update. The company emphasized both the productivity gains and the counterweight: Scientists were spending significant time validating AI output, accumulating hypotheses that still needed testing, and encountering bottlenecks in physical experimentation and clinical validation. Google said those constraints mean faster individual tasks may not immediately translate into more discoveries.

Productivity findings are substantial. Around three-quarters of surveyed researchers reported saving time through AI, and the average reported net saving was almost seven hours per week. Researchers said much of that time went back into research. The paper also reports that about 68% perceived greater access to insights from other disciplines, while roughly 65% said AI had increased the breadth of their research agendas.

But the authors found that checking AI output consumes a meaningful share of those gains. Among scientists who reported saving time, about 89% said more than 10% of the saved time went into verifying, debugging, or fact-checking AI output. About 46% said more than one-quarter of their saved time went to that work. The authors describe this as a verification tax associated with the high value science places on reliable and correct results.

Microsoft is introducing new tools within its sovereign cloud offerings designed to give organizations tighter control over where their data resides, who can access it, and how critical systems operate, especially in light of AI becoming a bigger part of enterprise infrastructure.

Cloud Security Alliance's 2026 Top Threats report ranks identity as the leading cloud security concern, followed by two new AI-related risks.

AI-powered vulnerability research is contributing to a major increase in security updates, raising concerns that IT teams could fall behind as patching demands grow, according to cybersecurity company ThreatDown.

In an Aug. 20 Dear Colleague Letter from Kirsten Baesler, assistant secretary of the United States Department of Education's Office of Elementary and Secondary Education, ED outlines new guidance to frame the national conversation around the educational value of technology in schools.

#7112. Normalizing ‘productive struggle’ could curb science anxiety

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Students can become too focused on finding “one right answer” when science is ultimately about experimentation and exploration, one educator says.

Whether they’re facing biology, chemistry or physics classes, not all students feel assured they’re a “science person” and can build enough competence and understanding to succeed, according to a former president of the National Science Teachers Association.

“I do think some students likely experience ‘science anxiety’ for a variety of reasons,” said Christine Royce, who’s also an education professor at Shippensburg University and co-director of the master’s in STEM education program.

One proximate cause is the high-stakes assessment system in the U.S., which instills a belief that there’s always one right answer to any given question, Royce said.

“That becomes problematic in science even more so, because while there are ‘more’ correct answers, science is also about exploration and experimentation,” she said. “They’re looking for, ‘What is the answer I’m supposed to give you?’ The problem is, depending on what data you’ve collected, that’s open to interpretation.”

But that open-endedness is often counterintuitive to how students have been prepared to face the “assessment era,” which can produce cognitive dissonance and result in anxiety, Royce said.

“If you’re looking at strategies to try to reduce science anxiety, one of the best things teachers can do is start to normalize the idea that productive struggle is part of the process,” she said. “They can learn from mistakes and move forward. That’s one of the best ways teachers can support students in science education.”

To strengthen that link, science teachers should help students recognize how what they’re learning about in the classroom is connected to the real world, Royce said.

“If they have a more grounded understanding of what’s going on because they can see it as part of their environment, they can see it as concrete, they will have a better ability to work through the connections,” she said. “If there’s no connection, students are, perhaps, guessing at answers."

On the other hand, "if there’s a connection, they’re using the information they already know to make predictions and draw conclusions,” Royce said.

Another method Royce recommends for helping students better understand science and reduce their anxiety around the topic is to encourage working with peers or in groups, to develop a wider notion of potential discoveries and answers they could gin up.

The dialogue between peers can help students make sense of what they're learning, she said. “That also helps them to realize that it’s OK to struggle. And while they may identify as not being ‘science people,’ science is part of our world, and it’s important for them to recognize that with science knowledge, they’re going to be better-informed about any field they go into.”

#7110. More Oversight: California Governor Proposes Independent Watchdogs Within AI Safety System

California Governor Gavin Newsom is proposing a new model of artificial intelligence regulation that would give independent organizations a formal role in evaluating the safety claims made by AI giants.

The effort could eventually include placing outside evaluators inside frontier AI laboratories and requiring companies to maintain an independently tested emergency shutdown mechanism, commonly described as a "kill switch."

Those requirements are not yet California law.

Newsom's Sept. 18 executive order directs state officials to study the proposals and recommend possible changes to existing law by Nov. 16. It does not immediately require OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, or other developers to install a shutdown mechanism.

The distinction matters because the phrase "AI kill switch" suggests a technical safeguard California has already imposed. For now, it is one element of a broader oversight system the state is assembling around the developers of its most powerful AI models.

Under the order, the California Government Operations Agency and the Governor's Office of Emergency Services must consult outside experts about whether the state should require large frontier developers to embed independent verification organizations in their laboratories.

The evaluators could conduct periodic audits and determine whether company safety frameworks and risk assessments meet adequate standards. They could also test whether an emergency shutdown mechanism would work as intended.

The order asks officials to consider expanding the definition of a reportable critical safety incident to include cases in which an AI system appears to evade controls or operate beyond its intended boundaries.

Newsom said the action was prompted by recent incidents involving AI agents defeating security restrictions, as well as the absence of comprehensive federal legislation.

"We're not waiting to act," Newsom said in the state's announcement of the order. "We're going to speed up our work on substantial and responsible AI oversight before it's too late."

The executive order builds on two laws Newsom signed on Sept. 9. Together, Senate Bill 813 and Assembly Bill 1405 begin regulating the organizations hired to inspect AI systems.

California's earlier frontier AI law, SB 53, requires large developers to publish safety frameworks and report specified critical incidents. The new laws begin addressing a separate question: Who checks whether those disclosures and assessments are reliable?

SB 813 requires the Government Operations Agency to develop standards for designating independent verification organizations, or IVOs. Those organizations must demonstrate that they have the expertise to assess AI risks and explain the measurements and methods supporting their conclusions.

Microsoft is introducing new tools within its sovereign cloud offerings designed to give organizations tighter control over where their data resides, who can access it, and how critical systems operate, especially in light of AI becoming a bigger part of enterprise infrastructure.

AI-powered vulnerability research is contributing to a major increase in security updates, raising concerns that IT teams could fall behind as patching demands grow, according to cybersecurity company ThreatDown.

#7103. New Industry Alliance to Work Toward Common Security Model for AI Agents

Okta has announced a coalition bringing together major cloud, security, data and application vendors to create a shared architecture for securing AI agents across enterprise environments.

The 12 founding members of the new Blueprint Alliance are AWS, CrowdStrike, Databricks, Docker, Google Cloud, Lovable, Okta, Proofpoint, Salesforce, ServiceNow, Wiz and Zscaler. GE Appliances and World Central Kitchen are participating as strategic advisors.

The effort expands an earlier Okta framework into an open, multi-vendor reference architecture intended to address a basic problem with enterprise AI agents: An agent can be created in one system, authenticated through another, access tools and data from several others, and be monitored by yet another security platform.

The Alliance is focused less on how agents are built and more on how organizations can govern them consistently across vendor boundaries.

The Blueprint Alliance architecture organizes agent security around four questions: Where are my agents? What can they do? What are they doing? How do I respond?

Those questions translate into several practical areas:

The reference architecture also covers development-related systems. Its whitepaper includes agent frameworks, code repositories, CI/CD environments, code assistants, tool registries, Model Context Protocol servers, and command-line tools among the components that may need to be discovered or governed.

That makes the initiative relevant to development teams deploying agents that interact with internal APIs, MCP tools, databases, SaaS applications, or other agents. The architecture calls for those connections and permissions to remain visible and controllable as agents move from development into production.

A major part of the Alliance's work will be testing how security information and controls can move between products from different vendors.

The initial architecture references several existing standards and protocols, including MCP for agent-to-tool interactions, the Open Cybersecurity Schema Framework for security telemetry, and the Shared Signals Framework and Continuous Access Evaluation Profile for exchanging risk information.

The goal is for one part of an enterprise stack to be able to detect a risk condition and pass that information to another control point that can act on it. Depending on the implementation, that could include revoking access or containing an agent whose behavior has changed.

The Alliance says members will build and test reference integrations and publish results as interoperability work progresses. Specific cross-vendor implementations and performance results were not documented in the launch announcement.

Founding member Lovable also published a developer-oriented explanation of its participation, describing organizations that may have internally built agents, agents imported through SaaS platforms and locally running agents that all need to be inventoried and governed under a common model.

The Alliance's whitepaper recommends that organizations begin with foundational capabilities such as unified logging, telemetry, and agent discovery rather than attempting to deploy every control at once.

The architecture is available for community use, and the Alliance says it will continue evolving as members test integrations and additional participants become involved. Timelines for individual vendor integrations have not been published.

For more information, check out the Okta announcement here.

David Ramel is an editor and writer at Converge 360.

#7098. Rigorous course participation rose after 2021 law, University of Illinois report finds

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Increases in dual credit and Advanced Placement participation in Illinois were particularly notable for Black students and students with disabilities.

Previous research shows links between success in programs like dual credit and AP, and greater college access and persistence.

Statewide, participation in dual credit courses rose by 17.1% overall in 2023-24 and another 9.1% in 2024-25, as compared with 1.6% and 6.9% in the two previous school years, respectively. Statewide participation in AP courses rose 6.6% in both 2023-24 and 2024-25, as compared with 2.3% the previous year and a participation drop of 5.9% in 2021-22.

While these and other gains were made in the immediate aftermath of the legislation, the study did not attempt to prove a causal relationship, said Sarah Cashdollar, associate director of the Illinois Workforce and Education Research Collaborative.

“Many other things are happening in the state that could be responsible,” she said, including amendments to the state’s Dual Credit Quality Act and higher state appropriations for dual credit and AP courses. “The state, the legislature, and all those who support it are throwing a lot at this issue in trying to improve participation in programs,” she said. “It’s hard for researchers to disentangle the effects of each of them separately.”

Nonetheless, the data describes a positive trend, said Meg Bates, IWERC director. “Certainly, it would have been a disappointment to see it go in the other direction,” she said. We’re excited to see it, and we’ll keep watching it. Hopefully we can do that kind of causal study in the future.”

The IWERC study bolsters other research that shows enrollment in rigorous courses is critical for underrepresented populations, Bates said.

For dual credit, the study found participation rates in 2023-24 increased from the previous year by 28.4% for Latino students and 25.6% for Black students. The year-over-year participation rate increase for Asian students was 15.6% and 13.9% for White students.

For AP, the increases were 9.2% for Latino students, 8.5% for Black students, 4.6% for White students and 3.4% for Asian students.

“Sometimes, we see a general trend that looks one way, but when you go underneath the hood, changes in certain students’ participation” runs counter to the trend, she said. “In this case, we got to see a little bit of both.”

However, she added, “That’s great, seeing more Black and Latino students, and more English learners. But because it’s going up for everyone, some of our gaps are widening.”

Those gaps should spur further research, Bates said, because educators and policymakers don’t want to see student experiences become more asymmetrical. “Following up with districts to understand the ‘why’ is very important.”

Cashdollar would like to see more research into the spikes in participation for English learners and Latino students.

“Was this group previously overlooked despite being capable of taking these classes — so the policy did what it was intended to do, and qualify more students?” she said. “A better understanding of all the different factors at play would be really useful.”

Bates believes a policy like automatic enrollment is uniquely equipped to target English learners. “Educating English learners can be costly for districts and can be hard to staff because you need bilingual educators,” she said.

This is a "nudge" to include English learners in this coursework," Cashdollar said.

"That’s a huge takeaway and something advocates are very excited about.”

#7094. AI Platform Behind 12,000 E-mail Breaches Dismantled

The platform moved quickly. Since launching in February, EvilTokens had been tied to more than 12,000 compromised inboxes across over 10,000 organizations worldwide, according to Microsoft. Its victims included businesses and institutions in financial services, healthcare, construction, real estate, wholesale distribution, and higher education.

Microsoft and its partners seized 50 websites used to operate EvilTokens and disabled more than 150 additional domains supporting the service. The Metropolitan Police Service in the United Kingdom also arrested two men on Sept. 11 in connection with the alleged operation.

At the center of EvilTokens was an AI chatbot designed specifically for cybercrime. Once attackers gained access to an inbox, the chatbot could analyze messages, map relationships, identify employees involved in payments, and recommend ways to impersonate trusted contacts.

Microsoft said the platform helped criminals determine "who to target, who to impersonate," and how to get the most money from a compromised organization.

Business e-mail compromise has traditionally required attackers to manually comb through messages, learn how an organization handles payments, and find the right person to impersonate. However, EvilTokens simplified the entire process by automating much of that work.

The tools could quickly sort through a stolen inbox, summarize or translate messages, find invoices and wire-transfer discussions, and identify the people who moved money. EvilTokens could then draft messages that sounded like they came from the victim. It packaged all of this with mailbox access, phishing tools, management dashboards, and even customer support.

The service was sold through Telegram for a $1,500 startup fee, followed by a $500 monthly subscription. Microsoft's complaint also described a bulk e-mail tool that cost $600 a month and a more advanced e-mail delivery platform priced at $1,000 a month.

To break into a target, EvilTokens relied on device-code phishing. Victims were sent to a real Microsoft sign-in page and persuaded to enter an authentication code. The login process looked legitimate because it was, but the code quietly authorized a session controlled by the attacker.

That also made cleanup more complicated. The attackers stole OAuth tokens and authenticated sessions instead of passwords, so a password reset alone might not remove their access. Administrators also needed to revoke the stolen tokens and active sessions.

According to Microsoft's complaint,  EvilTokens used fake Microsoft 365 security notices and other branded templates to direct users into the device authentication flow. Once inside, attackers could use Microsoft Graph and other APIs to search and monitor a mailbox.

The company also found that AI played a role in creating EvilTokens itself. Investigators said much of the platform appeared to have been "vibe coded," with its developers using AI to quickly build and expand the service. EvilTokens reportedly drew on multiple AI models, including improperly integrated services from Groq and OpenAI.

The court authorized Microsoft to take control of or disable domains connected to the operation. Microsoft worked with Cloudflare, Coinbase, OpenAI, Railway, SpyCloud, the Shadowserver Foundation, and TRM Labs on the disruption. Health-ISAC joined the case because healthcare organizations were among the targets.

This marks the Microsoft Digital Crimes Unit's 40th court-authorized disruption and its first targeting an end-to-end, AI-enabled cybercrime service.

EvilTokens' infrastructure may be offline, but the model behind it is unlikely to disappear. The operation showed how AI can turn a stolen inbox into an immediately useful fraud playbook, allowing less-experienced criminals to launch attacks that once required considerable time and skill.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog post.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

#7092. COVID learning recovery falls on states’ shoulders, CRPE report says

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Only about 1 in 10 students are in districts that are back to prepandemic achievement levels in math and reading, per the Center on Reinventing Public Education.

Pandemic-related learning recovery has "failed" for most students, and now states need to take the lead in accelerating academic achievement and driving improved student outcomes, according to a new report from the Center on Reinvening Public Education.

Only 10.5% of students are in districts that have returned to prepandemic achievement levels in both math and literacy. No states have returned to prepandemic attendance levels, according to the report. And with a downsized U.S. Department of Education shifting more responsibilities to the states, "all eyes [are] on states at a very pivotal moment," said Robin Lake, director of CRPE and co-author of the report. Lake was speaking at a Nov. 9 webinar on the report's findings, along with co-author Chelsea Waite, a research principal at CRPE. The report was released last month jointly with Arizona State University's Mary Lou Fulton Teachers College, where CRPE is housed.

To improve student outcomes, state leaders should investigate gaps between their policies and how they are implemented — including systems for measuring their progress and accountability performance. Then, they could prioritize plugging those gaps by paying special attention to the students furthest behind, including those with disabilities and multilingual learners, the report said. While academic performances varies widely among states, CRPE's analysis shows that every state has room for improvement. The analysis found that only 13 states have clear, specific, statewide goals for student learning that is messaged across multiple documents and webpages, said Waite.

South Carolina, for instance, has set a goal for 75% of its students to perform at grade level by 2030. "A clear goal is the first piece of a clear strategy," Waite said. "So without a clear goal, there's no way to tell whether a state is doing something that adds up to anything, and nobody can hold the state to that goal if it's not made public."

The CRPE report explores how state education policies, initiatives and reforms break down along political lines. The center found partisan divides in state-led instructional reform for math and literacy. For example, eight of the 12 states with “strong” policies for science of reading teacher preparation programs were categorized as red states, and 13 of 18 states with math laws were Republican-led.

On the other hand, student well-being policies — such as initiatives that touch on counseling, suicide prevention and school safety — are "led almost entirely by Democrats," the report said. Of the 17 states that have policies giving mental health days that can count as an excused absence from school, three are red states, eight are blue and six are purple.

The report found that states' academic achievement spanned the political spectrum, even though a "popular narrative" is that blue states outperform the others.

As far as how midterm elections will influence state education policy, that's a "big unknown," said Waite. The analysis was conducted before the November elections.

The report examines state policy adoption and implementation in four core areas:

Waite said the CRPE report is not claiming a certain combination of reforms will drive improved student outcomes. But, she said, early evidence suggests that "taking on several interconnected reforms at once does really seem to matter.”

#7089. St. Louis school board votes to close 9 schools

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The move comes as the Missouri district faces declining enrollment. Its board of education may consider other closures in the future.

Before the 4-3 vote to close the nine schools, St. Louis board members discussed the reasons for the move, including a need to consolidate resources by shuttering underenrolled schools and to improve student outcomes. Some board members blamed private school choice and historic underinvestments in local public schools for the position the district finds itself in.

The vote took place during a marathon meeting where board members considered several other recommendations to the district’s Future Ready SLPS initiative. That plan addresses financial stability, academic improvements, operational efficiencies and boosting attendance.

The recommendations also cover school repurposing, grade level configurations, school designations, school start times, and expanded career and technical education opportunities. Many of the proposed changes would begin in the 2027-28 school year, with additional transitions planned for future years.

At Tuesday's meeting, board members decided to decline, postpone or gather additional information on some recommendations.

In a Nov. 11 message to the community Superintendent Myra Berry acknowledged that school closure decisions are "deeply personal."

"Our schools hold relationships, memories, and a powerful sense of belonging," Berry said. "Throughout the development and discussion of Future Ready, we heard concerns, disagreements, and different convictions about the best path forward. Those perspectives deserve our continued attention, and the voices of our community must help inform the work ahead."

Berry added that the district will now focus on turning the approved changes into "thoughtful, carefully coordinated plans" by working with the affected school communities, preparing for transitions and communicating with the community about the changes.

Earlier this year, the Missouri Department of Elementary and Secondary Education downgraded St. Louis Public Schools to "provisionally accredited." The classification does not affect the operations of schools, Berry said in an undated message.

But it does mean the state "expects continued progress and sustained improvement in areas we have already identified and begun addressing — academic performance, financial stewardship, operational stability, and organizational culture," Berry said.

#7084. Proposed SNAP rule would threaten nearly 2M children’s school meal access

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

A proposed rule by the USDA would mean those children would no longer be categorically eligible for free school meals, despite 97% of them still qualifying.

The changes would come as a result of the USDA eliminating “broad-based categorical eligibility,” which is a state option that streamlines SNAP applications for households that already qualify for other assistance programs. More than 40 states and the District of Columbia use this option.

Eliminating this eligibility "may result in some level of increased administrative burden on states, a reduction in SNAP benefit spending, and associated effects on school meals eligibility," the USDA acknowledged in its proposal.

In addition to impacting school meal eligibility, the department estimates that approximately 42,000 children could potentially no longer receive the Summer Electronic Benefit Transfer beginning in summer 2029. The SEBT allows $120 in grocery benefits per eligible school-age child when school is out for summer.

The USDA’s new proposed rule would further exacerbate challenges for school meals, as well as students' benefits for summer meals, according to the Food Research & Action Center.

"Eliminating BBCE would reduce access to free school meals, making children hungry at home and at school," said FRAC in a Nov. 10 statement. "Children who lose SNAP are no longer automatically eligible for free school meals."

It would also increase administrative work for schools, such as those who were previously eligible for Community Eligibility Provision, the organization said. CEP helps high-poverty schools provide universal access to free school meals. The organization said it is concerned the impact could be “far worse than projected."

The public comment period on the proposed rule closes Dec. 10. The rule has so far received over 200 comments as of Thursday.

School districts have faced a slew of budget and eligibility challenges related to school meal programs under the second Trump administration.

In fall 2025, a prolonged government shutdown caused school systems and government agencies to provide outreach and extra supports for low-income families and children affected by the likely expiration of benefits.

Earlier that year, the Republican-led One Big, Beautiful Bill Act that went into effect July 2025 was estimated to reduce SNAP participation by roughly 2.4 million people in an average month between 2025-2034, putting school meals for children from those households at risk as they lost direct certification for free school meals.

The OBBBA changes also impacted CEP for districts. In Illinois, for instance, 47 fewer schools and 15 fewer districts participated in CEP between the 2024-25 and 2025-26 school years.

In addition to a reduction in eligibility, school district leaders have been concerned about the impact of federal budget cuts on school meals.

In May, 74% of school districts were concerned about the effect on school meals from looming federal budget cuts to SNAP and Medicaid, according to a report released by FRAC.

And in a September report by AASA, The School Superintendents Association, a majority — 67% — of superintendents said current funding levels are moderately or significantly hindering school meal programs. Two in 5 leaders said they had to pull funding from elsewhere in their district budgets to cover the cost of feeding students last year, including tapping into their rainy day balances or deferring maintenance upgrades.

When free school meals are no longer available to all students, teachers report an increase in student hunger and behavior issues negatively impacting students' focus on learning, according to a study published in September in the Journal of Nutrition Education and Behavior. The study reviewed the impact of the end of nationwide, pandemic-era universal school meals in June 2022.

#7080. Redesigned Microsoft Copilot Will Build Apps and Work on Its Own

Micorosft has announced that it's redesigning Copilot, the company's all-encompassing AI assistant, so users can hand off complex tasks, build tools by describing what they need, and assign work to an agent that keeps going after they log off.

The new Copilot experience has three main parts: Home, Code, and Autopilot. It also brings Word, Excel, and PowerPoint into the Copilot app. For users, the pitch is simple: Start a task in one place and stay there as it grows from a quick question into a document, an app, or a longer-running project. Most of the new capabilities are still rolling out or in preview.

Home puts Chat and Copilot Cowork together. Chat remains the place for quick answers, research, and drafts. Cowork handles work a user wants to delegate, such as preparing a briefing or pulling together a response to a request for proposals. Microsoft said Copilot eventually will route requests to Chat, Cowork, or Code on its own.

Office in Copilot will allow users to create a Word document, Excel workbook, or PowerPoint deck without leaving that experience. The files remain editable in the regular Office apps, and colleagues can work on them at the same time. Someone building a presentation, for example, could draw on a workbook, draft the slides, and keep refining both files from within Copilot.

Code is aimed at people who need a tool but might never open a development environment. A user can describe a tracker, dashboard, app, or workflow in plain language and have Copilot build it. Microsoft calls these "small, purpose-built solutions." The feature uses the same underlying technology as GitHub Copilot and runs in a sandboxed environment. A separate Copilot Managed Runtime, now in preview, lets organizations host and share the resulting apps within Microsoft 365.

Then there is Autopilot, previously known as Scout. It is designed for work that does not end with a single response: following a discussion, checking in with stakeholders, or keeping a project moving over several days. "Give it a name, a role, and a goal," Microsoft said. Autopilot runs in the cloud and has its own identity, memory, and workspace in the customer's tenant. Microsoft said its actions are subject to permissions, auditing, and organizational controls.

Those longer-running tasks also change how customers pay for Copilot. In a separate pricing post, Microsoft said everyday work in Chat and Microsoft 365 apps will remain covered by a per-user subscription. Cowork, Code, and Autopilot will be billed separately through usage-based Copilot Credits. A Copilot user subscription will still be needed to access those features.

For enterprise customers, the features that charge by usage will stay off until an admin sets up a spending policy in the Microsoft 365 admin center. Microsoft said there will be no charges before then. Admins can set budgets for the whole organization or for specific groups, limit how much individual users can spend, and get alerts when costs approach a set amount. Reports will show who is using the features and which tasks are driving costs.

Microsoft is bringing Code and Copilot Managed Runtime under those cost controls alongside Cowork. Copilot Studio agents are expected to follow in October. Before turning the features on for more employees, IT departments will need to decide who can use them, how much they can spend and which tasks are worth the extra cost.

Home and Code will begin rolling out through Microsoft's Frontier early access program in the coming weeks. Code's Frontier rollout starts at the end of September, when Autopilot also expands to private preview.

For more information, visit the Microsoft site.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

Microsoft is introducing new tools within its sovereign cloud offerings designed to give organizations tighter control over where their data resides, who can access it, and how critical systems operate, especially in light of AI becoming a bigger part of enterprise infrastructure.

Cloud Security Alliance's 2026 Top Threats report ranks identity as the leading cloud security concern, followed by two new AI-related risks.

AI-powered vulnerability research is contributing to a major increase in security updates, raising concerns that IT teams could fall behind as patching demands grow, according to cybersecurity company ThreatDown.

In an Aug. 20 Dear Colleague Letter from Kirsten Baesler, assistant secretary of the United States Department of Education's Office of Elementary and Secondary Education, ED outlines new guidance to frame the national conversation around the educational value of technology in schools.

You'll build for two kinds of consumers: people and AI. Teammates increasingly answer their own questions by querying our semantic layer through Omni's AI and MCP connections to AI tools. That means your models, metric definitions, and documentation need to be clear and correct enough for an AI agent to use them without a data teammate in the loop.

This is a full-time individual contributor role for someone who likes being close to the business, enjoys turning messy operational data into numbers people trust, and is happy to work on both the models and the dashboards that sit on top of them. When Sales and Product have clear, reliable data, we help more creators build more valuable businesses with Kit.

You'll report to Sam Umachi, Head of Data (Linkedin).

Sales and RevOps are your primary partners, who will rely on the models and reporting you own. You'll also partner with our Product team on reporting for features like Visual Automations and on how creators use Kit.

Within our data team, you'll work alongside a Staff Analytics Engineer who owns org-wide semantic layer governance and modeling standards, a Senior Data Engineer who owns ingestion and pipelines, and a Senior Business Analytics Partner focused on revenue opportunities. This role has no direct reports.

First Week: Work through our onboarding curriculum in Notion and meet the data team, our Senior Revenue Operations Manager, the sales team, and your product partners. Get set up in our core tools: dbt Cloud, GitHub, Amazon Redshift, Omni, HubSpot, Linear, Hightouch and Slack. Set up your AI-assisted development environment and learn the shared AI workflows our data team uses. Open and merge your first small pull request so you learn our review and deployment flow early.

First Month: Map how HubSpot objects (deals, pipelines and stages, companies, contacts, and owners) flow into our dbt models, and document the gaps. Take over day-to-day ownership of the sales models and Omni dashboards that teammates have been covering. Audit current sales reporting and list where definitions differ or numbers don't reconcile. Get familiar with how commerce data (products creators sell and the revenue they earn through Kit) lands in the warehouse today. Agree with RevOps and Sales on the most important questions they need answered. Review teammates' pull requests and contribute to our shared SQL, dbt, and documentation standards. Share a written reflection with the team on what you've learned and where you'd focus first.

First Six Months: Ship and maintain well-tested, documented dbt models for core sales metrics, such as pipeline, deal velocity, stage conversion, win rates, and the sales-led funnel. Expose those metrics through the Omni semantic layer, working within the team's governance standards. Rebuild or refresh the core sales dashboards so pipeline reviews and forecasting conversations run on numbers that don't need manual reconciliation. Resolve competing sales metric definitions by writing down the options and tradeoffs, aligning with Sales and RevOps, and documenting the outcome. Model creator-level commerce data, such as products sold, revenue earned through Kit, and conversion, in partnership with data engineering. Write semantic layer descriptions, metric definitions, and documentation that let Omni's AI and MCP-connected AI tools answer routine sales questions correctly, and test them against real questions from Sales, RevOps and Product. Get Sales to the point where routine questions are answered through self-serve, whether in Omni dashboards, Omni's AI, or AI tools connected over MCP, rather than one-off requests.

First Year: Be the recognized owner of Kit's sales data, the person Sales and RevOps go to first and trust without second-guessing. Give our revenue analysis work the sales and commerce models it depends on, so deeper questions about pipeline health and creator earnings can be answered quickly, thereby enabling us to make creators more successful. Leave your domains well documented and well tested, legible to new teammates, stakeholders, and the AI agents that query our semantic layer. Help raise the team's modeling quality through code review, pairing, and sharing what you learn.

Deep dbt experience. You build modular, tested, documented dbt projects, and you're comfortable with incremental models, snapshots, macros, CI, and scheduled jobs in dbt Cloud or similar.

Advanced SQL. You reason carefully about grain, joins, and window functions, and you write queries that are readable and performant on a cloud warehouse.

Data modeling for sales and GTM. You can model deals, pipeline stages and their history, ownership changes, and funnel conversion into clean, reusable structures. You are opinionated on Kimball standards and when a concept should be a fact or dimension.

CRM data fluency. You know how sales data behaves in a CRM, including stage changes, backdated edits, and custom properties. HubSpot is preferred, but other CRM experience (e.g. Salesforce) applies just as well. We care most that you recognize common CRM patterns.

AI-assisted development. You use AI code editors and agents (such as Cursor, Claude Code, Codex, or similar) every day, and you validate their output with tests, query results, and review before anything ships.

Clear written communication. You explain metric definitions, tradeoffs, and data caveats in plain language that Sales, RevOps, and Product partners understand.

BI and semantic layer experience. You can build dashboards and semantic models in Omni, Looker, or a similar BI tool, and you design metric definitions that people and AI tools can both query correctly.

Git-based workflows. You keep pull requests small and well described, and you give thoughtful code review.

Nice to have: Redshift performance tuning (sort and distribution keys), Python for scripting or API pulls, reverse ETL, and experience with product event, billing, payments, or ecommerce data.

Around 7 or more years in analytics engineering or data engineering where you personally shipped production data models.

Several years of hands-on work building and maintaining production dbt projects.

Working directly with sales teams and RevOps, and owning reporting they relied on to run the business.

Owning a data domain over time and evolving its models as the business and its questions changed.

Working at a SaaS company, ideally one with both a self-serve and a sales-led motion. Commerce, payments, or creator economy experience is a plus, not a requirement.

Working on a small or remote team where you were trusted to make decisions independently.

Ownership and follow-through. You treat your data domains as yours. You notice problems before stakeholders do, close the loop on requests, and finish what you start.

Judgment. You match rigor to the decision at hand, know when a quick answer is enough and when a request should become a durable model, and say so clearly.

Flexibility. You're glad to step outside your primary area when another team needs help, and you balance that work against your core commitments openly.

Teach everything you know. You document as you go, pair generously, and help stakeholders understand the data well enough to use it on their own.

Work in public. You share progress, open questions, and decisions in writing where the team can see them, including what didn't work.

Creator success is our success. You remember who's on the other end. A wrong number in a pipeline review means Sales spends time on the wrong creators, and that shapes how carefully you work.

Do it with excellence. You test your work, review it carefully, and don't ship a number you can't defend.

Candor with care. You're comfortable pushing back when a request won't answer the real question, and you do it in a way that builds trust.

Needing detailed specs or a highly structured environment before you can start.

Discomfort with ambiguity. Some of our sales and commerce definitions are still evolving, and you'll help settle them.

Needing several layers of approval to make modeling decisions within your domain.

Wanting to work only on dashboards, or only on pipelines, rather than across the transformation and BI layers.

Wanting a strictly fixed scope. Sales comes first, but you'll pick up Product or other work when the business needs it.

Wanting mostly greenfield work. Much of this role is improving sales models and dashboards that already exist.

Preferring to take tickets as written rather than partnering with stakeholders on what they need.

Being skeptical of AI's capabilities, or using AI without good judgment and accountability.

Expecting a large data team with dedicated specialists for every part of the stack.

#7076. AI Leaders Warn of Risks at United Nations Security Council Briefing

The heads of OpenAI, Anthropic, and Hugging Face recently joined AI researcher Yoshua Bengio at the United Nations headquarters in New York on September 23 to warn the UN Security Council about the security risks of increasingly capable AI.

They did not offer a single plan for managing those risks. Bengio called for licensing and independent safety reviews. OpenAI's Sam Altman proposed common standards that countries could adopt through their own laws. Anthropic's Dario Amodei urged AI developers to slow down when safety work cannot keep pace. Hugging Face's Clément Delangue warned that limiting access to capable systems could leave defenders at a disadvantage.

France, which held the Security Council's rotating presidency, convened the meeting on AI and international security. The discussion exposed a difficult question for governments: how to oversee technology that crosses borders when countries disagree about who should set the rules.

Bengio, co-chair of the UN's Independent International Scientific Panel on AI, challenged the idea that competition forces companies to keep building more powerful systems. "The race is not a law of nature: it is the product of choices," he said in his published remarks. "They've told us they would slow down if they could. They can."

He called on developers to show independent experts that frontier systems are safe to train and deploy. He also proposed incident reporting, licensing, and liability insurance. Bengio identified catastrophic misuse, concentrated power, and the loss of human control as risks that require international cooperation.

Altman acknowledged the danger of moving too quickly. "We have unilaterally slowed down in the past. We will do so in the future," he said, according to remarks published by OpenAI. Decisions about AI's direction "cannot be made by labs in San Francisco alone," he added.

Altman proposed complementary national and international standards to measure frontier AI capabilities, assess risks, and test safeguards. He said countries should be able to compare evidence and verify commitments. He also called for faster reporting of serious incidents and secure channels for sharing emerging threats. Such standards should accommodate both open and closed models without favoring established companies, he said.

Amodei made a more direct commitment about the pace of Anthropic's releases: "We will slow down as much as necessary," he told the council. He identified two principal concerns: the misuse of AI to develop biological weapons and the possibility that advanced systems could outstrip developers' ability to control them.

The Anthropic chief proposed placing outside evaluators inside AI companies with access comparable to employees. He suggested that governments begin with a narrow agreement against using AI to make biological weapons, then develop ways to evaluate models and verify commitments across borders. He also called for common testing standards and a system for notifying other countries of significant AI incidents.

Delangue drew on a July incident in which Hugging Face disclosed an AI agent cyberattack. He called for stronger monitoring and disclosure, but argued that safety also depends on giving defenders access to capable tools. The central danger, he said, was "asymmetry of powerful AI" between attackers and defenders, large companies and everyone else, and wealthy countries and the rest of the world. His team used an open-source model while responding to the attack, he said.

The U.S. government opposed a broader international regulatory role. White House science adviser Michael Kratsios told the council that countries should develop their own ability to use and govern the technology. In the U.S. statement, he rejected new global governance structures and used the phrase "superintelligence, formerly known as AI," adopting President Donald Trump's terminology from his General Assembly speech the previous day.

The four AI briefers differed on how far oversight should go and how widely capable systems should be available. They agreed on a narrower point: Governments need better ways to understand what advanced AI systems can do and to detect when safeguards fail.

John K. Waters is the editor in chief of a number of Converge360.com sites, with a focus on high-end development, AI and future tech. He's been writing about cutting-edge technologies and culture of Silicon Valley for more than two decades, and he's written more than a dozen books. He also co-scripted the documentary film Silicon Valley: A 100 Year Renaissance, which aired on PBS.  He can be reached at [email protected].

While security teams have invested heavily in e-mail protection, endpoint security, and identity controls, new research from Fortra suggests one challenge remains difficult to solve: users.

Microsoft has shut down EvilTokens, a cybercrime service that used AI to help attackers break into e-mail accounts, study their victims, and build convincing financial scams.

Avantis Education, the creator of ClassVR, has introduced two new immersive learning libraries for its Eduverse+ platform: EscapeRooms, a math suite for K-8 students, and LingoLab, a Spanish and English language learning suite for students in grades 6-12.

Artificial intelligence is saving scientists significant amounts of time, but researchers are giving some of those gains back by checking the technology's work, according to a new study involving Google, Google DeepMind, and MIT.

Class CrunchLabs, the suite of free curriculum resources from NASA engineer turned YouTube science communicator Mark Rober, has released its first two courses on YouTube.

Designed in partnership with the National Science Teaching Association, the standards-aligned "Carnival of Collisions: Contact Forces" and "Mission MagLev: Electric & Magentic Forces" courses offer free video lessons, guided activities, and supplemental teaching resources for grades 6-8.

Class CrunchLabs was launched last year to offer free hands-on science curriculum resources including teaching guides, classroom slides, student readings and handouts, assessments, and instructional videos featuring Rober alongside "Science Bob" Pflugfelder, celebrity guests, and student co-hosts. The initiative was backed by a $7.2 million grant to create the CrunchLabs.org Foundation, a nonprofit "dedicated to revolutionizing science education in a way that promotes critical thinking and inspires lasting curiosity and confidence in STEM," according to a news announcement at the time.

The organization plans to roll out additional courses on YouTube over time, with the ultimate goal of posting more than 1,000 science videos in 34 languages.

#7066. OpenAI Getting Rid of Custom GPTs and Moving to Plugin Architecture

OpenAI has announced it will retire Custom GPTs and move the specialized workflows built around them to a new plugin architecture spanning ChatGPT and its Codex coding agent.

Custom GPT retirement is currently scheduled for Dec. 11, 2026, although the company says timing and migration availability can vary by account and workspace.

For affected Enterprise workspaces, OpenAI's current migration schedule targets Oct. 26 for ending creation of new Custom GPTs. Qualifying Enterprise customers with approved deferrals can continue using them until Feb. 11, 2027.

OpenAI has changed its UI to make GPTs less prominent, and when users choose to try creating a new Custom GPT, they are now prompted with an option to create a plugin instead.

Rather than maintaining separate customized versions of ChatGPT, OpenAI is shifting customization toward plugins that can package reusable instructions, reference material, and connections to outside services.

Under OpenAI's plugin architecture, a plugin can include one or more skills containing instructions and resources for repeatable workflows, a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server exposing tools and external systems, or both. ChatGPT and Codex share a universal plugin directory, although some capabilities can remain specific to a particular environment.

For organizations, that makes the change more than a simple renaming exercise. A company could package recurring workflows into skills while using MCP servers or connected apps to give those workflows access to enterprise systems and data.

OpenAI says creators will be able to migrate an existing GPT through the My GPTs interface. The GPT's instructions become a skill in the replacement plugin, while knowledge files are copied into plugin reference files and connected apps can be carried over.

Not everything transfers automatically. Existing GPT conversations do not migrate, the selected model does not carry over, and Custom Actions may have to be rebuilt using an available app or a custom MCP server.

Sharing also changes. Migrated plugins start private, existing GPT users do not automatically receive access to the replacement, and prior sharing settings do not carry over.

OpenAI is also cautioning organizations against assuming that migration will preserve behavior exactly. "A migrated plugin may respond differently," the company says, recommending that users retest familiar prompts, reference-material use, required output formats, and integrations before relying on the replacement.

That warning has contributed to pushback from some GPT builders, who argue that plugins do not fully reproduce the dedicated and predictable environment of a Custom GPT. Other complaints have focused on sharing, migration complexity, and differences in how migrated workflows use instructions and reference material.

Despite those concerns, OpenAI is clearly consolidating around plugins as its common mechanism for extending its AI products. Its developer documentation now describes plugins as packages users can "discover, install, share, and publish" across ChatGPT and Codex, replacing the separate-custom-assistant model with reusable AI capabilities that can be combined with enterprise tools and services.

For more information, go to the OpenAI site.

David Ramel is an editor and writer at Converge 360.
